## Pakistan — English, Grade 6 (file: g06.pdf)

# Pakistan English — Grade 6 (PCTB) — Whole-Book Reading Draft

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed:** Cover: “ENGLISH 6”. Title page: “ENGLISH / GRADE 6”.
- **Publisher / board as printed:** “Punjab Curriculum and Textbook Board, Lahore” (cover); title page: “PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE”.
- **Curriculum basis as printed:** “Based on Single National Curriculum 2022” (cover and title page); title page adds “ONE NATION, ONE CURRICULUM”.
- **Year / edition / ISBN:** No publication year and no ISBN are printed. The imprint page (PDF p.5) is marked “Experimental Edition”. It names the author as Mr. Abdul Majid (M.A English), supervision Ms Yusra Raees (S.S English, PCTB), Director (Manuscripts), PCTB Farida Sadiq, Deputy Director (Graphics) Syeda Anjum Wasif, design by Khuram Shahzad / Qaiser Amin, and “Prepared by: Urooj Publications, Khalid Business Centre 6-Urdu Bazar, Lahore”. Reviewers: Muhammad Suleman (English lecturer), Afshan Mushtaq (Master Trainer, Punjab Education Foundation), Ms Ayesha Zafar (Content Writer), Ms Beenish Khalid (English Teacher, Beaconhouse School System).
- **PDF page count:** 129 PDF pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 5. Verified on foliated pages throughout: PDF p.6 = printed p.1 (Unit 1 opener), PDF p.7 = printed p.2, PDF p.129 = printed p.124 (Review 3). Front matter (cover, title page, two Table of Content pages, imprint) is unfoliated.
- **Folio range:** Printed pp.1–124.
- **Cover note:** the front cover carries a word-cloud of the book's grammar contents (“Adjective, Adverbs of Frequency, Position of Preposition, Direct and Indirect Speech, Paragraph, Pronunciation, Transitive Verbs, Degrees of Adjectives, Active Passive, Common Noun, Proper Noun, Intransitive Verbs, Adverbs”).

## 2. Architecture

- Front matter: cover, title page, then a **two-page Table of Content** unlike Grade 7's. The first contents page (PDF p.3) lists Unit, Page, Themes, Listening and Viewing, Listening and Speaking. The second (PDF p.4) continues the same units in parallel columns: Reading Comprehension, Vocabulary, Grammar, Punctuation, Writing. Then the imprint page.
- **12 units + 3 Review Exercises.** Review Exercise 1 is printed p.46, Review Exercise 2 p.91, Review Exercise 3 p.124 (the last page of the book).
- **No Preface, no separate Teachers' Guide chapter, no IPA chart and no back matter** — teaching guidance is instead embedded in every unit as boxed “For Teacher:” / “For Teachers:” notes at the foot of pages. The book ends abruptly with Review 3; there is no closing page after printed p.124.
- **Unit template:** a unit opener page carrying a long “Students Learning Outcomes” list (far more detailed than Grade 7's, often 10–17 outcomes quoting curriculum wording); then, in varying order: Pre-reading (picture discussion + “QUICK QUESTIONS”), Reading (the main text, with While-reading boxes), Reading Comprehension (a short word-meanings table + questions), Listening and Speaking (a printed dialogue to read and practise, plus group work/debate prompts), Vocabulary, Grammar, Punctuation, and Writing Skill(s). “LEARN IT!” sticky-note boxes give short rules in the later pages of a unit.
- Contents-vs-body warning: the Table of Content's unit titles for Units 8–10 do **not** match the body (see §7).

## 3. Unit-by-unit (printed page numbers)

- **Unit 1 — Seert-un-Nabi (pp.1–13).** Theme: Ethics and Values. Reading: a life of Hazrat Muhammad — birth in Makkah, Hazrat Khadija and the Syria caravan, Maysarah's report, “Al Sadiq” and “Al Amin”, the letter to Heraclius of Byzantium, the Bani Makhzum theft incident (cited “(Sahih al-Bukhari 3733)”). Listening/Speaking: Dawood helps an old man whose shopping bag tore — dialogue to read and practise. Vocabulary: **compound words** (snowflake, backbite, copyright); **homophones** (weather/whether, seen/scene, hair/hare, herd/heard, there/their) with a choose-the-word exercise (aloud/allowed, ate/eight, fair/fare…). Grammar: **nouns** — common, proper, collective, concrete, abstract; post-modifiers after nouns; nouns plural in form but singular in meaning (news, scissors, glasses, tweezers, pants, jeans); sentence patterns — SV (intransitive) and SVO (transitive). Punctuation: capital letters. Writing: topic sentence and supporting sentences; write a 9–10-line paragraph about a proud moment, after filling a mind map about Hazrat Muhammad.
- **Unit 2 — Eid-ul-Fitr (pp.14–26).** Theme: Festival and Cultural Events. Reading: Eid-ul-Fitr — Chand Raat, mehndi, fitrana, Salat ul Eid, Eidi, gratitude; headings in the text include the run-together line noted in §6. Listening/Speaking: Usman and his mother and the Eid shopping-list dialogue; a class discussion on junk food at Eid. Vocabulary: **homographs** (resign/resign, wind/wind, lead/lead) and homograph pronunciation practice (bass, minute, firm, tear, watch) plus dictation. Grammar: gender nouns — masculine, feminine, common, neuter, sorted from pictures; **personal pronouns** — subjective, objective, possessive in a table; interrogative pronouns; demonstrative pronouns (this/that/these/those, near/far, singular/plural); SVA (subject-verb-adjective) pattern; **tenses — simple present, simple past and simple future**, each with affirmative, negative and interrogative structures. Punctuation: exclamation mark. Writing: narrative paragraph — a model Independence Day speech-competition paragraph, then write about meeting your best friend, with Planning / Drafting / Reviewing stages set out on p.26.
- **Unit 3 — The Lake Isle of Innisfree (pp.27–35).** Theme: Nature. Reading: W. B. Yeats's poem complete in three stanzas, credited “By William Butler Yeats”. Listening/Speaking: Roshan and Babar discuss Multani mangoes; group discussion “Fruits are essential in a healthy diet”. Vocabulary: **root words with affix** — prefix vs suffix, root words, with (mislabelled — see §6) example tables; write words with super-, anti-, pre- and with -ment, -ness, -able. Grammar: **articles a / an / the**; **reciprocal pronouns** (each other / one another) and **reflexive pronouns** (-self/-selves); SVA-adverb pattern (SVAdv) and SVN (subject-verb-noun) pattern — the SVN example table's third column is headed “Adverb” (see §6); **present continuous tense** with affirmative/negative/interrogative structures. Punctuation: full stop, question mark and exclamation mark choice exercise. Writing: narrative writing — write a narrative on the most beautiful place you have ever visited.
- **Unit 4 — Major Shabbir Sharif Shaheed (pp.36–45).** Theme: Role Models. Reading: biography — born 28 April 1943 in Kunjah, Gujrat; Sword of Honour; 1971 war, Sulemanki Sector, Jhangar post and the Sabuna Distributary; martyrdom on the night of 5/6 December; the only soldier to receive both Nishan-e-Haider and Sitara-e-Jurat. Reading Comprehension adds **similes** with a meaning/sentence table (as clear as a crystal, as busy as a bee…). Listening/Speaking: Azhar meets his father's friend Mr. Rahman, a lieutenant — dialogue; class discussion on a student's aims. Vocabulary: **phrasal verbs** (blow up, carry on; also “get angry” listed — see §6) and **idioms** (lend a hand, it's raining cats and dogs, kill two birds with one stone). Grammar: **adjectives** and adjective order/types (quantity, quality, size, shape, colour, origin); **pronoun-antecedent agreement**; simple and compound sentences; **past continuous tense**; **gerund and participle**. Punctuation: commas in a paragraph. Writing: what a book blurb is and how to write one; write a blurb for the pictured book, and an explanatory paragraph from a poster (“Don't make excuses. Make improvements!”).
- **Review 1 (p.46).** One page only: word meanings (reliability, summon, martyrdom, accomplished, pavements, fearlessly); questions on Units 1–4; circle-the-correct-meaning items built on homographs/polysemy (desert, number/numb, wound, dove); write an essay on “Importance of sports and games” using a given topic sentence.
- **Unit 5 — Harmony in Society (pp.47–55).** Theme: Peaceful Co-existence / Peace Education. Reading: Haziq and Ahmad befriend Jasim, a new neighbour's son who was paralysed by polio and bullied at his old school — inclusion of a differently abled child. Listening/Speaking: Hafsa and Vajiha talk about fables vs science articles (likes/dislikes dialogue). Vocabulary (in the grammar pages): literal and non-literal meanings. Grammar: **degrees of adjectives** — regular and irregular (many/more/most, far/further/furthest, bad/worse/worst; well–better–best); transitional devices for addition (besides, along with, in addition to, moreover, furthermore); **relative pronouns** (that, which, who, whom, whose) and **relative clauses** in the following unit pages; clauses vs phrases (independent/dependent clauses; noun, verb, adjective, adverb and prepositional phrases); **present perfect tense** with affirmative/negative/interrogative structures. Punctuation: the “Four Rules for using Commas”. Writing: correct spellings of mis-spelt multi-syllable words (establishment, beautiful, uncomfortable… printed in distorted forms for correction), analogies, and write a story on “My best friend cannot walk” using multi-syllable words.
- **Unit 6 — First Aid Saves Life (pp.56–67).** Theme: Crisis Awareness, Management and Risk Reduction. Reading: Tina, a medical student, performs CPR on a woman who has collapsed in the road — 30 chest compressions, rescue breaths, ambulance, the doctor's praise; CPR is explained in the text. Listening/Speaking: a teacher introduces a speech competition (Waqar, Sharjeel); group topic “The Importance of English conversation in our daily life”. Vocabulary: **idioms** again (a fuller table: bread and butter, down to earth, hit the sack, no pain no gain). Grammar: **main verb and helping verb**; forming adjectives from nouns (-y, -al, -ial, -ish, -ic); **comparison clauses and relative clauses**; **past perfect tense** with structures; **use of *for* and *since*** (for + period, since + starting point, with example tables). Punctuation: parentheses ( ). Writing: **informal letter writing** — parts of an informal letter (address, date, salutation, body, closing, signature) with a labelled model letter from Kaleem to Saad about a father's Covid-19 recovery; write a birthday-invitation letter or a letter to parents about a new school.
- **Unit 7 — Corruption (pp.68–78).** Theme: Avoiding Social Evils. Reading: the corrupt minister Mr. Shan — bribery, a bridge built with poor material collapses, his son is injured, and a fake doctor (whose certificate Shan had signed) is to operate on the son; Shan repents. Listening/Speaking: Amjad invites Kamran to a birthday party (dialogue); debate on how good deeds can lead to a good life. Vocabulary: **use of a dictionary** — parts of a dictionary entry (entry word, definition, guide words, pronunciation, part of speech, pronunciation guide, origin) with a labelled dictionary-page picture; dictionary questions. Grammar: **modal verbs** — a full function table: can/cannot/could (ability), may/may not (permission), might (possibility), would/will/can (request), should (suggestion/advice), must/need to/have (obligation), would/will (invitation/offer), mustn't/can't (labelled “probation” — see §6); conjunctions/transitional devices used for reason (because, so, for); **adjectival phrases**; **past perfect tense** again, with notice-the-structure and change-to-negative/interrogative exercises. Punctuation: punctuate a paragraph about Ayesha and a cat. Writing: **formal letter** — rules and a labelled model letter from Sadia Khan to the secretary of ABC Society requesting street lights; write a formal letter to the police inspector about thefts in your area.
- **Unit 8 (body) — Overpopulation — a Dilemma (pp.79–90).** Theme: Population Education. (Contents calls this unit “Importance of Science & Technology” — see §7.) Reading: Danish and his grandfather on a hot, dusty night — the city then and now, “we add 227,000 more people to the planet every day”, resources, migration, poverty, and solutions (education, gender equality, family planning). Listening/Speaking: Innaya's solar-system model for a science exhibition (dialogue with her mother); debate on the three R's (Reduce, Reuse, Recycle). Vocabulary: **synonyms and antonyms**, thesaurus/dictionary page work, and a list of dictionary abbreviations (Memo, AM, PM, AD, BC, BCE, Email, Etc, Vet, Though, cause/coz, Cellphone, Psycho, Math, PE, Cc, Bcc, Re:, Till, No., E.g., i.e., RSVP, PTO, CV). Grammar: **prepositions of position, time, movement and direction**; conjunctions and transitional devices for purpose (so that, in order to, so as to), place (where, wherever) and choice (either…or, neither…nor); **adverb phrases**. Punctuation: **apostrophes** — singular possessive, plural possessive, and possessives not ending in s (women's, men's); the plural example is misprinted (see §6). Writing: write an essay on “Environmental Pollution” using a mind map; explain a four-picture road-safety sequence in at least three paragraphs.
- **Review 2 (p.91).** One page: word meanings (gloomy, devastated, unrestrained, slanted, bribery, toxic); questions on Units 5–8 (differently abled persons, first aid, corruption, overpopulation); make sentences with would, must, could, might, can't; describe your favourite vacation covering all five senses.
- **Unit 9 (body) — Education is my Right (pp.92–100).** Theme: Gender Equality and Equity. (Contents calls this unit “Overpopulation — a Dilemma” — see §7.) Reading: Mr. Akbar, a newly appointed teacher, cleans a derelict school; Ameena, a scrap-picker's daughter who reads under a light pole, gives first aid for a snakebite from what she read in books; the landlord funds a girls' school. Listening/Speaking: Maria asks Nazia how to write a letter; discussion “Role of women in sports”, then write to a principal asking him/her to introduce sports for female students. Vocabulary: **figurative language** — simile, metaphor, personification, hyperbole, onomatopoeia, defined in a table with a choose-the-type exercise. Grammar: **compound coordination and complex sub-coordination conjunctions**; compound sentences and complex sentences with their formulas. Punctuation: add apostrophes. Writing: fill a mind map about your favourite personality and **write a biography**, with an elements checklist on p.100 (chronological order, pronoun-antecedent agreement, transitional devices…).
- **Unit 10 (body) — Importance of Science & Technology (pp.101–109).** Theme: Science and Technology. (Contents calls this unit “Education is my Right” — see §7.) Reading: an essay-text on science and technology in daily life — machines, transport, communication and the “global village”, smart classes and the Covid-19 pandemic, medical devices, space and Mars. Listening/Speaking: Arham and Sultan discuss future professions (software engineer, teacher); group work on advantages of technology. Vocabulary: dictionary meanings — **denotative and connotative meanings**, with a blue/cool example table. Grammar: **sentence and its types** — declarative, exclamatory, interrogative, imperative, each defined with examples; classify-the-sentence exercises. Punctuation/Writing (combined pages): rewrite an eagles paragraph with correct capitalisation and punctuation; **writing speech bubbles** — write a dialogue in speech bubbles (two friends discussing a mathematics test; a father and son on the benefits of technology).
- **Unit 11 — A Poem by a Soldier (pp.110–116).** Theme: Patriotism / National Pride. Reading: a poem on the creation of Pakistan, credited “By Dr. Tafazzal”, beginning (on p.111) “Who knew that the day, / Of 14th of August,”. Listening/Speaking: Zubair and Basit discuss a late essay assignment; group exchange on “How Can One Be a patriot?” or “The importance of time”. Vocabulary: **use of a thesaurus** — how a thesaurus differs from a dictionary, with a labelled thesaurus entry for *technology*. Grammar: **direct and indirect speech** — rules for direct speech, rules for changing direct into indirect, pronoun changes, and full tense-change charts (present simple → past simple … past perfect → remains unchanged; future forms: “‘Will’ changes into ‘would’”, etc.). Punctuation: capitalise and punctuate a paragraph built from the Unit 8 overpopulation text. Writing: **essay writing** — introductory paragraph, main body, concluding paragraph (an essay is “at least three paragraphs”; a full-length essay/story is given as “(350-400 words)”); a Rashid Minhas essay is dissected with the three parts labelled; write an essay on “My father is my hero”.
- **Unit 12 — Good Citizen (pp.117–123).** Theme: Participatory Citizenship. Pre-reading: a traffic-rules poster, “Obey Traffic Rules And Signs / To Prevent Road Accidents”. Reading: Shiza and Tania clean their street, plant trees, spot strangers casing a neighbour's house, call the police helpline and warn neighbours to lock their inner doors; the thieves are arrested. Listening/Speaking: Nabeela asks her teacher's permission to submit an application (dialogue about classroom manners); group work on responsibilities of students in a classroom. Vocabulary: **onomatopoeia** (boom, tick tock, ding dong; drip, pitter-patter, splash, dribble, plop). Grammar: **changing direct speech into indirect speech** again, this time restricted to present tense → past tense, with worked tables and conversion exercises including questions (“The policeman said to the boy, ‘where do you live?’”). Punctuation: correct a paragraph about Ms. Maryam and Usman with appropriate punctuation and capitalisation. Writing: narrative paragraph writing repeated from Unit 2 — Planning / Drafting / Reviewing — write about an incident when you felt low and hopeless, and about a time someone or something filled you with happiness and hope.
- **Review 3 (p.124).** The book's final page: word meanings (luxurious, ease, vigilant, bow, war zone, imbibe); questions (figurative language, favourite innovation in science, a soldier's death, cleaning surroundings); change four direct-speech sentences into indirect speech; write any five similes and use them in sentences.

## 4. Grammar / vocabulary progression summary

- **Tense system taught (the widest of the three books):** simple present, simple past and simple future together in U2; present continuous in U3; past continuous in U4; present perfect in U5; past perfect in U6 and repeated in U7; *for/since* with the perfect in U6. Indirect-speech charts in U11–U12 additionally **name** present/past/future perfect continuous forms, but only as items in a conversion table — they are never taught as tenses in their own right.
- **Foundations first:** nouns and their five kinds, SV/SVO patterns and capitalisation (U1) precede gender nouns, all the pronoun families and patterns SVA/SVAdv/SVN (U2–U3), then adjectives, antecedent agreement, simple/compound sentences, gerund/participle (U4), degrees, relative pronouns/clauses, clauses vs phrases (U5), main/helping verbs and comparison clauses (U6), modals (U7), prepositions and adverb phrases (U8 body), compound/complex sentences (U9 body), sentence types (U10 body), and reported speech as the capstone (U11–U12).
- **Vocabulary strands:** compound words and homophones (U1), homographs (U2), affixes/roots (U3), phrasal verbs, idioms and similes (U4), literal vs non-literal meaning (U5), idioms again (U6), dictionary anatomy (U7 body order), synonyms/antonyms and dictionary abbreviations (U8 body), figurative language (U9 body), denotation/connotation (U10 body), thesaurus (U11), onomatopoeia (U12). Word-meanings tables accompany every reading text.
- **Writing progression:** topic-sentence paragraph (U1) → narrative paragraph with planning/drafting/reviewing (U2, repeated U12) → narrative (U3) → book blurb and explanatory paragraph (U4) → story and analogies (U5) → informal letter (U6) → formal letter (U7) → mind-mapped essay and picture-sequence writing (U8 body) → biography (U9 body) → dialogue in speech bubbles (U10 body) → three-part essay (U11).

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 129 PDF pages:

- **Passive voice as a topic:** never taught. The words “Active Passive” appear in the cover word-cloud, but no unit presents active vs passive, gives a passive formation, or sets a passive exercise. This is the book's most conspicuous gap, because the cover advertises it.
- **Conditionals:** no conditional type (zero/first/second/third/mixed) is taught or named anywhere. *If*-sentences occur in examples and in a transitional-devices table (“Let me know if you go to school”) with no conditional rule.
- **Future perfect, future continuous, present perfect continuous, past perfect continuous as taught tenses:** none is presented with a structure or exercise. The perfect-continuous names occur only as rows in the U11/U12 indirect-speech tense-change charts.
- **Adverbs as a word class, and adverbs of frequency:** never taught as a lesson, although “Adverbs of Frequency” and “Adverbs” appear in the cover word-cloud, and adverb phrases / the SVAdv pattern are taught (U3, U8 body). No frequency-adverb table (always/usually/often…) is given.
- **Articles beyond U3, determiners/quantifiers, question tags, inversion, subjunctive, causative:** not taught.
- **Relative clauses** are taught (U5–U6) and **reported speech** is taught at length (U11–U12) — recorded here to show the check was made; these are *not* negatives for this book.
- **Exam preparation:** none. No board/exam-format paper, no marks scheme, no timed or model-paper section. The three Reviews are one page each of mixed revision questions.
- **Answer keys:** none. No exercise, review or conversion table exercise is answered in the book (the worked examples inside grammar tables are the only completed items).

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments only)

- Printed p.58 (U6 reading): “She rolled the laddy on to her side” — *laddy* printed for *lady*.
- Printed p.59 (U6 reading): “train those who were interested in first aid and CRP” — *CRP* printed for *CPR*, in a unit whose subject is CPR.
- Printed p.75 (U7 modal table): the function column for mustn't/can't reads “probation” — printed for *prohibition*.
- Printed p.17 (U2 reading): “AnotherkeyelementofEid celebrations” — words run together with no spaces at the start of a paragraph.
- Printed p.23 (U2 grammar): the simple-present negative example is printed as a question — “We do not play outside after school every day?” — with a question mark on a statement example.
- Printed p.30 (U3 vocabulary): the affix example tables are headed the wrong way round — a table headed “Root word / Prefix” lists *less* → restless, careless, hopeless (suffix formations), and the “Audi” table likewise lists derived words under “Prefix”.
- Printed p.34 (U3 grammar): the SVN (subject-verb-noun) example table's third column is headed “Adverb” although its entries are *a doctor* and *a nurse*.
- Printed p.41 (U4 vocabulary): “get angry” is listed in the phrasal-verb table with the sentence “Maria gets angry at Ayesha” — not a phrasal verb.
- Printed p.70 (U7 reading): the While-reading box asks “Which kind of life did Mr. Shaan wish for?” while the story's character is spelled “Mr. Shan” throughout the text.
- Printed p.89 (U8 punctuation): the plural-possessive example repeats the singular form — under plural nouns the example is again printed “The bee's hive”, with the caption “(the hive belongs to more than one bee)”.

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **Watermark:** every content page carries a large diagonal watermark, “Web Version of PCTB Textbook / Not for Sale”, crossing text, tables and pictures throughout. It is a property of the web-version source, present on all 129 pages.
- **Contents/body mismatch (a defect of the printed book as reproduced in this file, not of the scan):** the Table of Content (PDF p.3) lists Unit 8 as “Importance of Science & Technology” (p.79), Unit 9 as “Overpopulation — a Dilemma” (p.92) and Unit 10 as “Education is my Right” (p.101). In the body the openers read: printed p.79 “Unit 8 — Overpopulation - a Dilemma”, printed p.92 “Unit 9 — Education is my Right”, printed p.101 “Unit 10 — Importance of Science & Technology”. The three titles are rotated against the unit numbers and page numbers; all other units (1–7, 11, 12) match their contents entries.
- **Folio continuity:** checked page by page — printed folios run 1–124 in sequence with **no missing, duplicated, truncated or blank pages** and no misbinding; unit openers fall at printed pp.1, 14, 27, 36, 47, 56, 68, 79, 92, 101, 110, 117 and the Reviews at 46, 91 and 124. Several pages are largely white by design (e.g. printed p.100, which contains only the biography checklist, and p.13).
- **Other marks:** no library stamps, handwriting or cropped margins were found. Page headers alternate “English 6” left/right as part of the design.

## Pakistan — English, Grade 7 (file: g07.pdf)

# Pakistan English — Grade 7 (PCTB) — Whole-Book Reading Draft

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed:** Cover: “ENGLISH 7”. Title page: “English / GRADE 7”.
- **Publisher / board as printed:** “Punjab Curriculum and Textbook Board, Lahore” (cover); title page: “PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE”.
- **Curriculum basis as printed:** “Based on Single National Curriculum 2022” (cover and title page); title page adds the slogan “ONE NATION, ONE CURRICULUM”.
- **Year / edition / ISBN:** No publication year and no ISBN are printed anywhere in the front matter. The imprint page (PDF p.3) is marked “Experimental Edition”. It names authors Shaukat Abbas (MPhil English) and Aamir Mahmood Awan (MA English), reviewers, supervision by Yusra Raees, Director (Manuscripts) PCTB Ms. Farida Sadiq, Deputy Director (Graphics) Syeda Shehzadi, designer Zahoor Ellahi, and “Prepared by: Gohar Publishers, 11 Urdu Bazar, Lahore”.
- **PDF page count:** 137 PDF pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 7. Verified on foliated pages throughout: e.g. PDF p.9 = printed p.2, PDF p.12 = printed p.5, PDF p.136 = printed p.129, PDF p.137 = printed p.130. Front matter (cover, title page, imprint, Preface/Teachers' Guide pp., Contents pp.) is unfoliated; Unit 1's opening page is printed p.1.
- **Folio range:** Printed pp.1–130. Every page also carries the footer “NOT FOR SALE (PESRP)”.

## 2. Architecture

- Front matter: cover, title page, imprint/authors page, a two-page Preface that doubles as a teacher's guide (“How to use this book?”, “Teachers' Guide — How to Lecture?”, lecturing method, questioning, brainstorming, discussion, role-play, investigating), then a two-page Contents table.
- The Contents table is itself a curriculum map: for each unit it lists Page No., Unit, Themes, Sub-Themes, Oral Communication Skills, Reading and Critical Thinking, Vocabulary & Grammar, and Writing Skills.
- **12 units + 4 Review sections.** Reviews fall after Units 3, 6, 9 and 12 (Review 1 printed p.29, Review 2 p.59, Review 3 p.91, Review 4 p.126).
- Back matter: a one-page IPA pronunciation chart (printed p.130) is the final page. There is no index and no glossary section separate from the per-unit glossaries.
- **Unit template (very regular):** a full-page unit opener with a “Learning Outcomes” box (“After completing the unit, students will be able to: …”); the reading text with Pre-reading, While-reading and Post-reading boxes in the margins; a “Glossary” table (Words / Meanings); then an “Exercise” in four lettered strands — **A Oral Communication Skills, B Reading and Critical Thinking, C Vocabulary & Grammar, D Writing Skills** — with yellow “Remember” theory boxes and occasional “Teacher's Note” boxes. Most reading exercises end by asking pupils to guess word meanings and then “compare them with their dictionary meanings”.

## 3. Unit-by-unit (printed page numbers)

- **Unit 1 — The Last Sermon of Rasoolullah (pp.1–11).** Theme: Ethics and Values / respect for rule and law, fairness. Reading: the Last Sermon in seven numbered sections (1. Life and Property of the Muslims; 2. Blood Revenge and Usury; 3. Rights of Women; 4. Worship of Allah; 5. Equality; 6. The Quran and The Sunnah; 7. Bearing Witness), with hadith citations. Oral: rhetorical questions — identify them and produce your own. Reading/critical: silent letters (circle them in *right, whom, listen, comb, honour…*); topic sentence; summary. Vocabulary & Grammar: prefixes and suffixes (mis-, pre-, un-, over-; -ly, -ness, -ed, -er, -ful), root words, MCQs; pronoun–antecedent agreement; using a dictionary (guide words, entry word, accent mark, syllable dots, countable [C] / uncountable [U]). Writing: write an objective summary of the unit. Glossary: *sermon, sacred, entrust, blood revenge, deliberately, waive, committed, chastise, mission, superiority, deliver, righteousness*.
- **Unit 2 — Martyrs of Pakistan (pp.12–21).** Theme: Role Models. Reading: Major Tufail Muhammad (Nishan-e-Haider, Lakshmipur, August 1958) and Rashid Minhas Shaheed (PAF, 20 August 1971). Oral: role-play a dialogue between a school counsellor and a student about choosing a school/college and preparing for interview. Reading: literary vs non-literary texts, MCQ quiz. Vocabulary & Grammar: main and helping verbs (primary auxiliaries be/do/have; modal auxiliaries listed); **simple past tense** — formation, regular -ed, negatives with *did not* / *was/were + not*; correct spelling choices (-able/-ible type pairs, e.g. *horrible/horrable*). Writing: process of writing a paragraph (pre-writing, research, drafting, revising, editing/proofreading); write paragraphs on “Nishan-e-Haider and its significance”. Glossary: *battalion, assign, evict, martyrdom, commission, valour, cockpit, absconding, wrest, deliberately, posthumously, reverence*.
- **Unit 3 — A Nation's Strength (Poem) (pp.22–28).** Theme: Patriotism / National Pride. Poem by William Ralph Emerson: wealth, swords and pride do not make a nation; brave, truthful men do. Oral: read and role-play a café dialogue (Shiza/Urooj), using a tag question in social conversation. Reading: answer questions, paraphrase a stanza. Vocabulary & Grammar: syllables and syllable division; **apostrophe** — possessive, omission/contractions, plural nouns; capital letters (in the contents map); **four types of sentences** — declarative, interrogative, imperative, exclamatory. Writing: argument — claim/evidence/conclusion; write an argument that only “Strong People make a Nation Strong”. Glossary: *defy, rust, foes, decay, throng, pride, shafts, a people, luster, flee, dare*.
- **Review 1 (pp.29–31).** Rhetorical questions from statements; rhyming-word matching; review questions on Units 1–3; matching meanings; silent letters; syllable counting/division; apostrophes; identifying sentence types; writing on “Rights and duties as a member of society” or a poem on “A patriot”.
- **Unit 4 — Quaid-e-Azam (pp.32–40).** Theme listed in contents as “Self, people and Place” / knowing people of different religions and ethnicity. Reading: biography of Muhammad Ali Jinnah — Two-Nation Theory, Lucknow Pact 1916, Fourteen Points, Round Table Conferences, 1937 elections, “Demand for Pakistan”. Oral: role-play a dialogue between two friends about summer vacations, practising non-verbal gesture (per contents). Vocabulary & Grammar: **articles a / an / the**; adjectives — attributive vs predicative position; making adjectives from nouns and verbs with suffixes (-able, -ful, -less, -ous, -ed, -ish, -ly, -ive, -ant, -ent, -ible); **adjectival phrases**. Writing: dictation of unit words; process writing, brainstorming and structuring a mind map; write an essay on Quaid-e-Azam from a given mind map. Glossary: *proponent, conflicting, electorate, pact, aspiration, sentiment, embark on, legislature, brutally, unreflective, tremendous, impact, renaissance, hostile, inevitable*.
- **Unit 5 — Smog (pp.41–49).** Theme: Environmental Education / introduction to climate change. Reading cast as a classroom dialogue between a Teacher and Students: what smog is (smoke + fog), Punjab worst affected, Lahore AQI “about 550” against a safe level “up to 50”, causes (vehicles, industry, brick kilns, crop residue), health effects, and an 11-point list of safeguards. Oral: practise a dialogue about stress (Mumtaz/Anjum). Vocabulary & Grammar: **personal pronouns** (subjective/objective); **homophones** (to/too/two; low/law, sight/site, word/world, through/throw, check/cheque); apostrophe (') used in three ways, including plurals of letters, numerals and signs (“two a's”, “3's, 7's, 8's”). Writing (per contents): write a paragraph; in the body the writing task is an essay on “The causes of pollution and its solutions”. Glossary: *smog, deforestation, dementia, hazardous, residue, industrialisation, particle, issue, fog, sulphur*.
- **Unit 6 — Pakistani Female Mountaineer (pp.50–58).** Theme: Gender Equality & Equity / role of women in the development of society. Reading: Samina Khayal Baig — first Pakistani woman on Everest (2013), K2 passage at the start, Seven Summits list, UNDP goodwill ambassador 2018, gender-equality expedition with her brother Mirza Ali. Oral: listen to the lesson read aloud and discuss the pictures in class. Vocabulary & Grammar: **punctuation** surveyed in full — capitalisation, parenthesis, exclamation mark, full stop, apostrophe, comma, semi-colon, colon, hyphen, dash, quotation marks — with rewrite/punctuate exercises including an unpunctuated “rainbows” paragraph; **present perfect simple tense** — structure for positive, negative and interrogative; **use of *since* and *for*** (with a note that *for/since* are also used in “the perfect simple and perfect continuous tense”). Writing: write an essay on any “Pakistani sports personality” in chronological/sequential order. Glossary: *mountaineer, peak, elevation, expedition, demonstration, approximately, empowerment, scale (v), forecast, emulate, incredible, trekking, recognize, achievement, inequality*.
- **Review 2 (pp.59–61).** Discussion questions using underlined words; matching words to meanings (summit, particle, lucrative, ambassador, emission, smog…); review questions on Units 4–6; *for/since* gaps; homophone/near-homophone word pairs in sentences (peak/peek, hoard/horde, advice/advise, moral/morale, adopt/adapt, accept/except); dictation list; punctuate a farmer-and-tiger passage; short essay on “The struggle to save the environment” or “An inspirational leader”.
- **Unit 7 — Eid-ul-Adha (pp.62–71).** Theme: Festivals & cultural events / festivals and global cultural events around the world. Reading in family-dialogue form (Zainab and Saliha ask their parents): the sacrifice of Hazrat Ibrahim and Hazrat Ismail, division of meat into three parts, Eid-ul-Fitr vs Eid-ul-Adha, and a boxed list of “sunnahs (habitual practices) of the day of Eid”. Oral: practise a dialogue between two friends (Hamza/Ali) about a trip to the Kelantan International Wau Festival in Malaysia. Reading: **point of view** — first person vs third person, with pronoun lists. Vocabulary & Grammar: **homophones and near homophones** (whether/weather, seen/scene; loose/lose); word-bank confusables (then/than, affect/effect, sense/since, an/and); **position of adjectives** and adjective order (a sidebar lists the order: number, opinion, size, shape, age, condition, colour, pattern, origin, material, purpose). Writing: **word analogies**; write a short dialogue between two friends on choice of career. Glossary: *festival, approaching, command, celebrate, commemoration, sacrificial, environment, clarify, calendar, differentiate*.
- **Unit 8 — The Wise Old Woman (pp.72–83).** Theme: Peaceful co-existence / peace education; diversity and acceptance of diversity. Story retold by Yoshiko Uchida (1921–1992) (credited on the opener): a cruel young lord banishes everyone over seventy-one; a farmer hides his mother; her wisdom solves Lord Higa's three impossible demands (a thousand ropes of ash, threading a curved log, a drum that sounds without being beaten); the lord repents. Oral: read and role-play a mother–daughter cooking dialogue (Rabia/Mother, fried rice). Reading: figures of speech — simile, metaphor, personification, hyperbole, onomatopoeia — identification exercise; fact and opinion; a short “The Simpsons” paragraph with MCQs on synonyms/antonyms. Vocabulary & Grammar: syllables and syllable division again; **idioms and proverbs** — matching idioms (“I am feeling blue”, “in a pickle”, “a piece of cake”…) and proverbs (“No pain, no gain”, “A stitch in time saves nine”…). Writing: make words with -able, -ible, -ably, -ibly; write a doctor–patient dialogue, the doctor insisting on a healthy diet. Glossary: *arrogant, haughtily, decree, banish, murmur, disobey, terrible, commotion, soak, bewilderment, moan, amazement*.
- **Unit 9 — Think Not All is Over (Poem) (pp.84–90).** Theme: Personality development / handling emotions effectively. Poem by Harriet Beecher Stowe (credited at the foot of the poem): autumn/winter despair answered by spring, God's care and a “morning hour”. Oral: asking questions to get personal information — greetings and question prompts, with model answers to complete. Reading: pathetic fallacy, the poet's use of “Think not” as a negative statement saying something positive, symbols of sadness/hope. Vocabulary & Grammar: **homographs** (row, close, wind, record, present, tear — noun/verb); **degrees of adjectives** in full — positive, comparative, superlative; regular rules (-er/-est, -r/-st after -e, y→i, doubling), irregular (good/better/best, bad/worse/worst), more/most for longer adjectives, and absolute adjectives that have no degrees (unique, perfect, round). Writing: **paraphrasing** — rules and technique; paraphrase the first two stanzas. Glossary: *wailing, shiver, blossom, shalt, weary, dreary, cherished, endure*.
- **Review 3 (pp.91–93).** Discussion on “Eid Day”; identify metaphor/simile/personification; fact or opinion; an unseen Nepal paragraph with MCQs; near-homophone choices (to/two, bare/bear, sea/see, their/there, son/sun); comparative/superlative table; analogies from a word box.
- **Unit 10 — The Young Boy's Adventure (pp.94–102).** Theme: Adventure / difference between thrill seeking and adventure. First-person flying memoir credited “(John Glenn with Nick Taylor)”: eight-year-old “Bud”, his father Herschel of the Glenn Plumbing Company, Lindbergh's Atlantic crossing, and a first biplane flight from a grass-field airport outside Cambridge. Oral: a teacher–students dialogue on attentive listening and turn-taking. Vocabulary & Grammar: **modal verbs** in detail — would, need not, could, might, shall, must, ought to, dare, each with a stated use; **abbreviations and acronyms** — a table of abbreviations (adj., adv., Dec., Dept., Govt., Dr…) and acronyms (CAP, NASA, ADA, PARCO, CED, NAB, OPEC, WASA) with the explicit (incorrect — see §6) statement that WHO is an abbreviation of World Health Organisation. Writing: read a sample formal acceptance letter (Naeem Mansoor to Mr Saleem, Director HR, accepting a writer's job at “Rs. 80,000 per month”) and write a formal letter. Glossary: *light-hearted, curious, investigate, curiosity, unbounded, plumbing, spot (v), cockpit, biplane, goggles, transatlantic, barely, elated, suspended, dogfight, progressive*.
- **Unit 11 — Pakistani Culture (pp.103–117).** Theme: National Pride / understanding National Pride (Language, Dress, Culture, Food, Arts & Crafts). Long survey reading: a definition of culture quoted from Allama Iqbal; then Punjab (dress, cuisine, sports, festivals, dance and music, customs, literature/Sufi poets), Balochistan (dressing, festivals, music, food, sports), Sindh (language and literature — Shah Abdul Latif, Sachal Sarmast; lifestyle and food), and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa (food, poetry, clothing, sports including buzkashi, explained as “buz” = goat, “kasha” = dragging/pulling). Oral: read and practise a Rabia/Fiza dialogue about a missed school function. Vocabulary & Grammar: **sentence patterns SVO, SVOO and SVOC**; direct and indirect object; finding and marking keywords in comprehension questions; **future perfect tense** — structure for positive, negative and interrogative, with choose-the-form and change-to-future-perfect exercises; **phrases, clauses and sentences** — independent vs dependent clauses; dependent (subordinate) clauses introduced by *which, that, who, when, if, before, after, because, unless*. Writing: **editing and proofreading** — a worked, hand-corrected model paragraph about building the pyramids, then edit and rewrite a paragraph about a student named Wajid. Glossary: *custom, ceremony, spiritual, ingredient, conviction, aspect, heterogeneous, exclusive, shrine, passionately*.
- **Unit 12 — Role of the Media (pp.118–125).** Theme: Media / media as a source of instant knowledge, social media ethics. Reading: media as “the fourth pillar of democracy” and “mirror of modern society”; print media, electronic media, social media (Facebook, YouTube, Twitter, Instagram named); the “dark side” — mudslinging, propaganda, ratings; a closing plea for privacy and human improvement. Oral: read and practise a long Doctor/Attendant dialogue about a father's colorectal cancer, tests, surgery and radiation therapy — an oral exercise unrelated in topic to media. Vocabulary & Grammar: **compound nouns** and their plural forms; correct-spelling choices (notebook/notbook, government/goverment…); pronouns surveyed — personal, demonstrative, interrogative, reciprocal (*each other / one another*), indefinite. Writing: **summary skills** repeated from Unit 1; read a passage on electric trolley cars/trams in the United States and the decline of the train system in North America, give it a title, state its main idea and summarise it; write a salesman–customer dialogue about buying a car. Glossary (two tables): *communication, interaction, broadcaster, propagation, influence (v), motivating, momentum; defamation, revolution, constituent, mudslinging, propaganda*.
- **Review 4 (pp.126–129).** Role-play a social-networks dialogue (Azmat/Nusrat); future-perfect gaps; modal auxiliary choices; full forms of abbreviations/acronyms and the reverse; review questions on Units 10–12; compound-noun plurals; write a dialogue between a salesman and a customer about buying a mobile phone; summarise an unseen paragraph on exercise.
- **Back matter (p.130).** IPA pronunciation chart: consonants, short vowels, long vowels, diphthongs, each with an example word and transcription, closing with the instruction to consult the chart “for the pronunciation of words in all the units”.

## 4. Grammar / vocabulary progression summary

- **Tenses actually taught as systems:** simple past (U2), present perfect simple (U6, with since/for), future perfect (U11). Simple present, present continuous, past continuous, simple future and past perfect appear only incidentally in other books' patterns, in examples, or in the reported-speech-style tables of U11/U12 exercises — they are **not** presented as tense systems in this book (see §5).
- **Sentence grammar builds steadily:** pronoun–antecedent agreement (U1) → main/helping verbs (U2) → four sentence types (U3) → articles and adjective position/phrases (U4) → personal pronouns (U5) → punctuation survey and present perfect (U6) → point of view and adjective order (U7) → figurative language (U8) → degrees of adjectives (U9) → modals and abbreviations (U10) → sentence patterns SVO/SVOO/SVOC, future perfect, phrase/clause/sentence (U11) → compound nouns and the pronoun families (U12).
- **Vocabulary work is the book's backbone:** every unit has a printed glossary (roughly 8–15 words), a guess-then-check-dictionary exercise, and a word-formation or confusables strand that recycles across units — prefixes/suffixes (U1, U4), silent letters (U1), syllables (U3, U8), homophones (U5, U7), homographs (U9), idioms/proverbs (U8), analogies (U7, Review 3). Dictionary skills are taught explicitly in U1 and reused constantly.
- **Writing progression:** summary (U1) → paragraph process (U2) → argument (U3) → mind-mapped essay and dictation (U4) → paragraph/essay on pollution (U5) → chronological essay (U6) → dialogue and analogies (U7) → dialogue (U8) → paraphrase (U9) → formal letter (U10) → editing/proofreading (U11) → summary again plus dialogue (U12).

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 137 PDF pages:

- **Present perfect continuous:** never taught as a tense; it is only mentioned in passing inside the since/for note in U6 (“we use perfect continuous tense. e.g. It has been raining for two hours”) with no structure or exercise.
- **Past perfect:** never taught. It appears only as an example sentence form inside U11's future-perfect conversion exercise (“They have built… / We had learnt…”) — no rule, structure or name is given. (Contrast Grade 6, which teaches it.)
- **Past continuous, present continuous, simple present, simple future as named tense systems:** none is presented with a structure box in this book; verb forms from them occur in reading texts and exercises only.
- **Passive voice:** never taught — no active/passive distinction, no passive formation, no exercise anywhere.
- **Conditionals:** no conditional type (zero, first, second, third, mixed) is taught or named. *If*-clauses occur inside reading and clause examples only.
- **Reported / indirect speech as a system:** not taught in this book. (It is taught in Grade 6 Units 11–12; here U10's sample letter and U11's clause work do not cover narration change.)
- **Relative clauses as a named topic:** not taught. Relative pronouns appear only inside U11's dependent-clause examples (“a poem which is in English”) and U12's pronoun exercise (“I saw ___ at the bus stop but I don't know who he was”).
- **Gerunds / participles, question tags as a system, inversion, subjunctive:** not taught (a tag question is used once in the U3 dialogue — “It is a hot day, isn't it?” — without explanation).
- **Exam preparation:** none. There is no exam-format section, no past-paper or board-style paper, no marks scheme, and no timed practice. The four Reviews are ordinary revision exercises in the same A–D format as the units.
- **Answer keys:** none. No answers are printed for any exercise, MCQ or review in the book; the glossaries and “Remember” boxes are the only places where information is given rather than elicited.

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments only)

- Printed p.101 (U10, “Abbreviation and Acronym” box): “WHO is the abbreviation of ‘World Health Organisation’” — WHO is presented as an abbreviation in the same box that defines an acronym as a word formed from first letters and “pronounce[d] as one word” (its own NATO example), so the book's own example contradicts its own distinction.
- Printed p.122 (U12): the punctuation exercise directs pupils “(For rules of punctuation see page 60)” — the punctuation rules are in fact set out on printed pp.55–56 (Unit 6); no punctuation rules appear on p.60, which is Review 2.
- Printed p.51 (U6 text): “Samina Khayal Baig is the first Pakistani female mountaineer who created history by reaching the top of the 2nd highest peak in the world the 8611 metre high K2. She is the third Pakistani to climb Mount Everest.” — the K2/Everest claims are run together so the “first” attaches ambiguously.
- Printed p.95 (U10 text): the John Glenn excerpt begins mid-flow with “I especially enjoyed being with my father…” with no title, introduction or opening sentence for the narrator on the page; the unit opener (p.94) gives no source line beyond the author credit at its foot.
- Printed p.130 (IPA chart): several long-vowel symbols in the left column of the “Long Vowels” table are clipped/illegible in this printing (the symbol column for *father, sore, fur, tool* does not print cleanly), although the example words and transcriptions are readable.

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **Watermark:** every content page carries a large diagonal watermark reading “Web Version of PCTB Textbook / Not for Sale”, frequently crossing text, tables and faces in photographs. This is a property of the web-version source file, present on all pages checked.
- **Folio continuity:** checked page by page — printed folios run 1–130 in order with **no missing, duplicated, truncated or blank pages**; no page is misbound (unit openers, glossaries and exercises all fall where the Contents table puts them: U1 p.1, U2 p.12, U3 p.22, Review 1 p.29, U4 p.32, U5 p.41, U6 p.50, Review 2 p.59, U7 p.62, U8 p.72, U9 p.84, Review 3 p.91, U10 p.94, U11 p.103, U12 p.118, Review 4 p.126).
- **Other stamps/marks:** the footer “NOT FOR SALE (PESRP)” is printed on every foliated page (part of the book, not a copy defect). No library stamps, handwriting, scanning skew or cropped margins were found; a few pages are largely white space by design (e.g. the second half of printed p.130, and the lower half of the U10 opener, printed p.94).
