## Pakistan — English, Grade 8 (file: g08.pdf)

# Pakistan English — Grade 8 (PCTB) — Whole-Book Reading Draft

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed:** Cover: “ENGLISH 8”. Title page: “English 8” (the word “English” in red, the “8” below it; the word GRADE does not appear on this title page).
- **Publisher / board as printed:** “Punjab Curriculum and Textbook Board, Lahore” (cover); title page: “PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE”.
- **Curriculum basis as printed:** “Based on Single National Curriculum 2022” (cover and title page); title page adds “One Nation, One Curriculum”.
- **Year / edition / ISBN:** No ISBN is printed. The imprint page (PDF p.5, roman p.iv) is marked “Experimental Edition” and carries a printing table headed “Published By: Punjab Curriculum And Text Book Board Lahore. / Printed By: Master Book Depot.” with the row “DATE: NOV 2022” and print-run columns PMIU 991,301; PEF 190,991; PEIMA 8,353; MLWC 290; Total 1,190,935. NOV 2022 is the only date printed in the book's front matter. Authors: Rafiq Mahmood, Z.I. Farooqui, Mrs. Sabiha Saleem, B.A. Chishty, Mohammad Aslam. Reviewers: Ms. Zarqa Bashir, Dr. Zafar Iqbal, Ms. Parveen Din Muhammad. Supervised by Yusra Raees (Subject Specialist English); Director (Manuscripts) Farida Sadiq; Deputy Director (Graphics) Syeda Anjum Wasif; Designer Minal Tariq; Illustrator Ayat Ullah.
- **PDF page count:** 129 PDF pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** Printed page = PDF page − 7. Front matter is foliated in roman numerals (Content List pp.ii–iii, imprint p.iv, “About the Book” pp.v–vi). Unit 1 opens on printed p.1 (= PDF p.8). Verified throughout: PDF p.15 = printed p.8, PDF p.129 = printed p.122.
- **Folio range in this file:** Printed pp.1–122 — but the Content List promises a Review-03 on printed p.123, which is **not in this PDF** (see §7).

## 2. Architecture

- Front matter: cover, title page, a two-page **Content List** (Units / Themes / Oral Communication Skills / Reading Skills on p.ii; Vocabulary and Grammar / Writing Skills / Page # on p.iii), imprint (p.iv), and a two-page **“About the Book”** spread (pp.v–vi) that explains the unit machinery with annotated sample pages.
- The “About the Book” pages state the design explicitly: each unit's title is derived from its theme; each unit starts with **Talk Time** (a “one word icebreaking” activity); boxes include **Learning Outcomes, Activity, Teaching Point, Remember** (“to revise those concepts that are being taught repeatedly in almost each previous grade”), **Read Me** (explains the concepts being taught), and **Just a Reminder!**; a **Review** is given after a group of units “to assess students' learning”; and each unit is divided into four competencies — Oral Communication Skills, Reading Skills, Vocabulary and Grammar, Writing Skills.
- **12 units + 3 Reviews in the Content List.** Review-01 follows Unit 4 (printed p.42), Review-02 follows Unit 8 (p.85), Review-03 is listed at p.123 after Unit 12 — but is missing from this file (§7).
- **Unit template:** opener with Learning Outcomes + Talk Time; the reading text with Pre-reading and While-reading margin boxes and a Glossary table; Oral Communication Skills (usually a printed dialogue to role-play, plus an Activity); Reading Skills (A. Reading for Understanding — contextual vs dictionary meanings; B. Comprehension Questions); Vocabulary and Grammar (lettered sections A, B, C… with pink “Read Me” theory boxes); Writing Skills (lettered tasks, often ending with a “Proofreading and Editing / Peer Review” checklist). Unit 1 additionally ends with an “Exit Card” self-assessment (“I have learnt that ___ / I still need help with ___ / Rating my understanding” with three faces) — this Exit Card does **not** recur at the end of the later units.
- No back matter in this file: no IPA chart (contrast Grade 7), no index. In this copy the book simply stops at printed p.122.

## 3. Unit-by-unit (printed page numbers)

- **Unit 1 — Tolerance of the Rasoolullah (pp.1–9).** Theme: Ethics and Values. Reading (numbered paragraphs 1–5): tolerance defined; forgiveness of Abu Sufyan at the conquest of Makkah; Taif and the Angel of the Mountains (cited “(Al-Bukhari :3231)”); the last address (“Revenge for bloodshed … is forbidden”); Hazrat Ans Bin Maalik's ten years of service. Oral: a dialogue in which Asghar settles a pen quarrel between Aslam and Imran; role-play resolving a fight. Reading Skills: main idea and topic sentence of paragraph 1, fill-in-the-blanks from the text. Vocabulary & Grammar: **dictionary use** — steps, entry word, guide word, pronunciation, part of speech, definition (labelled dictionary page for *counter*); multi-syllable words and syllabic division; **prefixes and suffixes** (dis-, in-/im-, re-, mis-, un-, under-; -able/-ible, -ful, -less, -dom, -er, -ment, -ness). Writing: paragraph writing with a mind map (“Real Life Incident”); narrative writing using a story graphic organiser; proofreading and editing (peer review checklist); Exit Card. Glossary: *hardship, display, trouble, destroy, recognise, patience, forbidden, murder, revenge, custom, objected*.
- **Unit 2 — A Great Virtue (pp.10–21).** Theme: Peaceful Co-existence. Story: on a stormy night a pious old man in a one-bed hut shelters, in turn, a hunter, a farmer, and a mother with two small children; the farmer apologises for his selfishness. Oral: Majid borrows Naveed's pen — a polite-expressions dialogue. Reading Skills: comprehension MCQs; skim and scan; **elements of a story** — setting, character(s), plot (beginning/middle/ending), climax, resolution, theme — applied to the story in a plot diagram, and a character sketch of the old man from a mind map. Vocabulary & Grammar: **thesaurus** use; synonyms and **antonyms** from a thesaurus; **silent letters** (know, knot, campaign, write, wrath, wrist, comb, wrong, knife, assign, reign, lamb); **diphthongs and triphthongs** with IPA symbols (train /ei/, eye /ai/, boy /ɔi/, oval /əʊ/, owl /aʊ/, chair /eə/, ear /ɪə/, sure /ʊə/; dire, inspire, Ireland); **singular and plural nouns** — all five formation rules (+s, +es, y→ies, f/fe→ves, irregular); **countable and uncountable nouns**; punctuation — capitalisation, full stop, comma, question mark, exclamation mark and **quotation marks**, with a punctuate-the-paragraph exercise about the telephone. Writing: write a few good deeds; story writing — build a vocabulary bank and write a story with the moral “Do good, have good”. Glossary: *to step in, apologise, accommodate, harsh, shelter, shivering, terrible, hesitation*.
- **Unit 3 — The Twins (Poem) (pp.22–30).** Theme: Personality Development. Poem by Henry S. Leigh (Henry Sambrooke Leigh, 1837–1883; an “About the Poet” box is given): identical twins mixed up by the nurse, “My brother John was named me, / And I was named him”, one twin flogged for the other. A Remember box teaches **intonation**. Oral/listening: a prose retelling of **The Pied Piper** (credited “Andrea Diamond”) read aloud by the teacher, with oral questions and story-element identification. Reading Skills: comprehension; **figures of speech — alliteration, hyperbole, assonance** (the Pied Piper lines are used for examples). Vocabulary & Grammar: matching meanings; rewriting lines in simple sentences; **pronouns by case** (subjective, objective, possessive); **reflexive pronouns**; **pronoun antecedent agreement** (a Pied Piper paragraph is rewritten with wrong pronouns to correct); **indefinite (vague) pronouns**; **tenses — simple present** (habitual acts, general truths, usual acts, facts/schedules) and **present continuous** (now, temporary, arranged future), with a choose-simple-or-continuous exercise. Writing: **paraphrasing skills**; **summary skills** with summary-writing tips (third person only); paragraph on daily routine in the simple present.
- **Unit 4 — Let's Make Our Roads Safer! (pp.31–41).** Theme: Participatory Citizenship. Talk Time is a labelled specimen **newspaper report** (“Youth killed in a road accident in Lahore”, dated 8 October, 2018) with its features annotated (date of publication, headline, name of the reporter, image with caption, city, beginning/middle/ending). Reading: a road-safety essay — traffic-law violations, young victims, “almost 80% of traffic accidents take place due to mistake or negligence of drivers”, then headed causes: over speeding and rash driving; use of mobile phones while driving; not wearing seat belts; not wearing safety helmet; violating traffic signals; violating lane discipline; violating traffic signs; and a closing section on giving way to emergency vehicles. Oral: prepare a speech on saving lives by following traffic rules. Vocabulary & Grammar: **proofreading** (rewrite a deliberately corrupted copy of the speeding paragraph); **compound words** and their two base words; **degrees of adjectives** (positive/comparative/superlative table to complete); **forming adjectives from nouns and verbs** by suffixes; **articles — the, a and an** (including “there are occasions when no article is used”); **apostrophe** for contractions and possession; **tenses — simple past and past continuous** (including past continuous with always/continually for persistent past habits), with a simple-or-continuous exercise. Writing: paragraph on an unaddressed traffic rule; analyse a news accident report using a mind map (injuries, remedies, causes, treatment, scene); **write a newspaper report** in a provided template (newspaper name, date, headline, reporter's name, report, image). Glossary (two tables): *insecure, congestions, offence, penalise, negligence; urban, inclement, invoke, penalties, divert, prohibited, fatal, vulnerable, mandatory, violation, lanes, markings*.
- **Review-1 (pp.42–46).** A listening poem about water (Julia W. Wolfe) with oral questions; group talk on traffic rules; review questions on Units 1 and 4; an unseen passage on **Abdul Sattar Edhi** with comprehension questions and contextual meanings; encircle the diphthongs in a poem (“The Very Loud Mouse”); plural endings -s/-es/-ies table; sort words into countable/uncountable boxes; add pronouns to a Waniya/Mahrukh dialogue; verb-form gaps; articles gaps; split words into syllables; circle compound nouns; writing — a paragraph about a small effort that made you feel special (at least five adjectives) and a mind map on “Be Helpful”.
- **Unit 5 — Hazrat Umar (pp.47–56).** Theme: Role Models. Talk Time: read and discuss a poem, **“Prayer” by M. Athar Tahir** (an “About the poet” box: Pakistani civil servant, Tamgha-e-Imtiaz 1998, Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai Award 1990). Reading (numbered paragraphs 1–5): the second Caliph, “Faruq”; ten and a half years as Caliph; administrative reforms, courts, army, police, tax; justice (“Treat your servant as you would treat yourself”); the night rounds and the widow whose children were crying over a pot of water on the fire; his killing by “a non-Muslim named Feroze”. Oral: a Mother/Ali dialogue about not skipping namaz; group presentation on a blessing of Allah. Reading Skills: identify the theme of the text and of each paragraph (a Para 1–6 diagram — see §6); silent letter hunt in a short story. Vocabulary & Grammar: **dictation** (paragraph 2 of the unit); **idioms and proverbs** (better late than never, call it a day, under the weather, spill the beans, break a leg…; complete-the-proverb exercise); **kinds of sentences** — declarative, interrogative, imperative, exclamatory; **homographs** (rock, present, watch, right, bear in paired sentences); **homophones** (find the pair in each sentence: pale/pail, sea/see…); **tenses — present perfect and present perfect continuous**, with a full **for & since** spread (timeline diagrams and lists) and a perfect-or-perfect-continuous exercise. Writing: six-step summary writing applied to this unit's text; imagine yourself ruler of your country; essay on “Importance of Justice” with a mind map; proofreading and editing with a peer-review checklist that now includes subject/verb and pronoun/antecedent agreement. Glossary: *embrace, treat, condition, matter, judgement, commit, reign, swordsman*.
- **Unit 6 — Sports and Sportsmanship (pp.57–67).** Theme: Sports. Talk Time: discuss a “Sports Gala, 2022” advertisement poster. Reading (numbered paragraphs 1–11): sports and character; the Roman proverb “mens sana in corpore sano”; a definition of sportsmanship quoted from Brian G.; Pakistan's sports — hockey as national game (Olympics and World Cup record 1956–1986), cricket (World Cup 1992, World Twenty20 2009, Champions Trophy 2017), squash (Jahangir Khan “unbeaten even after 555 consecutive matches”, 17 World Opens / 12 British Opens as printed), women in sport (Sana Mir, Kiran Baloch's 242, Rushna Khan, Saba Aziz), and the Pakistan Sports Board (PSB) with “forty-one National Sports Federations”. Oral: Ahmad/Ali dialogue about weekend cricket plans; class discussion on the importance of sports. Reading Skills: fact and opinion; a “Super Star Fact File” activity. Vocabulary & Grammar: fill the missing words in a basketball paragraph (James Naismith, peach baskets, “Duck on a Flock” — as printed); **abbreviations and acronyms** (St., cm, vs; RADAR, SCUBA, NASA, LASER, SIM card, ZIP code expanded); **adverbs** — manner, time, place, with change-adjectives-to-adverbs and identify-the-adverb exercises; phrase vs clause; **adverb phrase** and **adverb clause**; **tenses — past perfect and past perfect continuous**, with a perfect-or-perfect-continuous exercise. Writing: essay on “My Favourite Sport” from a mind map; a dialogue between two friends about a cricket match; design a Sports Day advertisement. Glossary: *fundamental, adherence, showcasing, collaboration, ferocious, extraordinary, inauguration, prestigious, significant, consecutive, affiliated*.
- **Unit 7 — The Earth Speaks (pp.68–75).** Theme: Environmental Education. Poem by Lenore Hetrick (credited): the Earth gives the four seasons — “Four seasons I give you” — and asks that each gift be used “wisely, kindly and well”. Talk Time: discuss a litter-picking picture. Oral: group suggestions for keeping surroundings clean. Reading Skills: comprehension; **figures of speech — simile, metaphor, personification** defined with examples, find the personification in the poem; make your own similes from pictures. Vocabulary & Grammar: **capitonyms** (Turkey/turkey, March/march, May/may, August/august, Polish/polish, China/china); **denotations and connotations** (blue, wave, pour in a two-column table); **conjunctions and transitional devices** — co-ordinating, subordinating and **correlative conjunctions** (either–or, neither–nor, both–and, not only–but also, whether–or), with join-the-sentences exercises and a Band-Aid invention paragraph (adapted from *Why Didn't I Think of That?* by Web Garrison, as credited) in which transitional words are encircled; **tenses — simple future** (including the *going to* form) **and future continuous**; **modal verbs** — one definition box (must, shall, will, should, would, can, could, may, might) and a five-item fill-in, with no per-modal function teaching. Writing: summary of the poem; write a poem on the invention of the band-aid using figurative language; a “Fist to Five” self-assessment activity on tenses.
- **Unit 8 — Settling the Quarrel (pp.76–84).** Theme (Content List): Avoiding Social Evils. Talk Time is a whole poem about anger, **“Anger” by Debbie Palphreyman** (as credited), with oral questions (“CALM I can count to ten”). Reading: Haji Usman, a village conciliator near Faisalabad, settles the quarrel between Haroon (crops destroyed) and Salman (whose tractor swerved into the fields to avoid Haroon's sheep and smashed into a wall): Haroon pays for the tractor repair at once; Salman will help Haroon plough next year. Oral: Ijaz/Rehan apology-and-reconciliation dialogue, completed in a second part; complete a teacher–class dialogue orally using the bold phrases. Reading Skills: plot analysis activity; **cause and effect** (signal words listed: because, so, since, as a result, if…then, therefore, due to). Vocabulary & Grammar: **compound prepositions** (in front of, along with, instead of, at the top of, according to); **prepositional phrases**; **gerunds and participles** (gerund vs present participle, with an underline/encircle exercise); **direct and indirect speech** — the present-tense set: simple present → simple past, present continuous → past continuous, present perfect → past perfect, plus changes in modal verbs (can→could, may→might, will→would, shall→should), time expressions (now→then, yesterday→the day before…) and place/demonstratives (here→there, this→that), with ten conversion sentences. Writing: develop a story from the poem “Anger” with a suitable title and a butterfly graphic organiser; narrate a real incident in which you helped settle a quarrel. Glossary: *quarrel, conciliator, impartial, accidently, destroyed, bloodshed, portion, wage* (glossary spelling “accidently” — see §6).
- **Review-2 (pp.85–87).** A listening poem, “Taking A Tour in Hills” by Hifsa Ambreen; review questions on Units 5–8; a comprehension passage on the benefits of **reading** (“knowledge is power”), with an instruction to rewrite it using modal verbs other than *can*; identify sentence kinds; homophone pairs in paired sentences (pale/pail, through/threw, whole/hole, except/accept, there/their, peace/piece); use homographs in sentences (left, bat, ring, bow, letter); join sentence pairs with conjunctions; change five sentences into indirect speech; writing — a dialogue about a cricket match, and an essay on keeping surroundings clean with a mind map.
- **Unit 9 — Mountaineering (pp.88–95).** Theme: Adventure. Reading (numbered paragraphs 1–5): the fascination and danger of climbing; Tenzing Norgay and Sir Edmund Hillary, Everest 1953; training, institutions and equipment; **Muhammad Ali Sadpara**, who “had ascended eight of the world's highest mountains” and lost his life with two fellow climbers attempting K2 in winter 2021; oxygen, storms, frostbite and willpower. Oral: read tongue twisters (a), (b), (c) aloud and identify alliteration and assonance in them, recalling the definitions from “(Page# 26)”. Reading Skills: comprehension. Vocabulary & Grammar: synonyms and antonyms of the highlighted words; **noun phrase and noun clause** (definitions and identification exercise); **direct and indirect speech — the past-tense set**: simple past → past perfect, past perfect → remains past perfect, past continuous → past perfect continuous, with conversion sentences; **proofread** a deliberately corrupted copy of paragraph 2 of this unit's text. Writing: **sensory language** — sort words under the five senses (eye, ear, nose, tongue, hand pictures); write a descriptive essay on “Dangerous Sports” from a four-paragraph mind-map frame; proofreading and editing (peer review). Glossary: *fascinated, extraordinary, risk, conquer, feature, struggle, ledges, valuable, circumstances*.
- **Unit 10 — Dignity of Labour (pp.96–104).** Theme: Dignity of Labour. The unit opens not with a reading passage but with a **Talk Time play-dialogue**: Saad teaches his little sister Taniya to make a vegetable sandwich so their unwell mother can rest, with a full **Recipe Card** (ingredients and seven steps). Reading (numbered paragraphs 1–5): dignity of labour defined; farmers as “the backbone of every society”; Ahmad of Grade VIII whose father, educated at Faisalabad Agriculture University, gets bumper crops; uneducated farmers, losses and discouragement; the subcontinent as “Golden Sparrow”; respecting and educating farmers. Oral: Maryam/Shabnam dialogue about a Labour Day (1st May) poem competition. Vocabulary & Grammar: **antonyms** via a thesaurus; **action verbs, linking verbs and helping verbs** (three-column Read Me, classification exercises); **direct and indirect speech — the future set**: will → would, shall → should, with conversion sentences. Writing: write a recipe of your own (name, ingredients, procedure table); essay on rural-to-urban migration with a reasons/effects/solutions diagram; and — lettered “C” again (see §6) — a labelled model **informal letter** (Aslam to a friend, 12 August 2022, on an Independence Day speech) with Address / Date / Greetings / Body / Closing farewell / Signature labels, then write a letter to a younger sibling. Glossary: *backbone, cultivating, famine, casualties, starvation, exhausted, crop, bumper, appreciated, fertility*.
- **Unit 11 — Give to Your Country (Poem) (pp.105–113).** Theme: Patriotism / National Pride, Self, People and Places. Talk Time is a magazine-article spread, “MY COUNTRY AND ITS FEATURES”: Pakistan's flag explained, a “Facts to Know” box (National Language Urdu; Capital City Islamabad; National Dress Shalwar Kameez; National Sport Hockey; Population 207.7 million; Currency Rupee; Area 881,913 km²; Longest River The Indus River; Largest Lake Manchar Lake; Highest Mountain K-2, 8,611 m; Seasons), a map, “Top Five Places to Visit” (Swat, Hunza, Yarkhun, Kalash, Margala Hills) and “Fascinating Facts” (Tarbela Dam, the irrigation system, Gwadar port, Sialkot sports goods, Arfa Abdul Karim Randhawa). Reading: the title poem by Lenore Hetrick (credited) — “Give your country her due… / With patriotic words and deeds.” Oral: “Fold and Fly” — give and follow paper-glider instructions in pairs. Reading Skills: comprehension; a jigsaw “Gist Writing” activity on a paragraph about human competition. Vocabulary & Grammar: **transitive and intransitive verbs**; **adjective phrase and adjective clause** (identify each); **direct and indirect speech — reporting questions** (asked/inquired; *whether/if* when there is no interrogative word), with eight conversion questions; **punctuation — semi-colon and colon**, their uses and examples. Writing: summary of the poem; a magazine article about your native city; your own poem dedicating yourself to your country; proofreading and editing (peer review).
- **Unit 12 — Trees Plantation (pp.114–122, as far as this file goes).** Theme: Environmental Education / Climate Change. There is no separate reading passage: the unit's texts are a model **email** in Talk Time (Asjad Javed invites Fahad to visit “Azad Jammu and Kashmir”, with Gmail/Outlook/Yahoo icons shown) and two **diary pages** dated 21st March 2022 and 25th March 2022, signed “BB”, about National Tree Plantation Day at school, planting saplings with gardener Uncle Riaz, a class duty chart and a sapling-growing competition. Oral: Saima/Nasir dialogue about making pots from plastic bottles; formal and informal language — making and attending telephone calls (steps for both, complaint/inquiry/parent-calling-school role-plays). Reading Skills: dictionary meanings and ten comprehension questions on the diary. Vocabulary & Grammar: **sentences — simple, compound and complex** defined with a three-column example table; **direct and indirect speech — reporting requests and commands** (ordered/requested/commanded) and **exclamatory sentences in reported speech** (exclaimed with joy/sorrow/wonder), with large conversion exercises. Writing: write a diary page about a school trip; write an email encouraging a friend to plant saplings; read a formal letter to “The Managing Director, Punjab Curriculum & Textbook Board, Lahore” (in the task rubric it is called a letter “to the Director of Education”) complaining of the “Paucity of Textbooks in the Area of Allama Iqbal Town, Lahore”, identify its parts and contrast formal and informal letters; proofreading and editing checklist. **The unit — and this PDF — end here, at printed p.122.**

## 4. Grammar / vocabulary progression summary

- **The tense ladder is the book's spine and it is completed in stages:** simple present + present continuous (U3) → simple past + past continuous (U4) → present perfect + present perfect continuous, with for/since (U5) → past perfect + past perfect continuous (U6) → simple future + future continuous (U7), with modals added as a single box in U7. Eight tense forms are thus presented as systems with uses and discriminating exercises (“simple or continuous”, “perfect or perfect continuous”).
- **Reported speech is the second spine, spiralled over five units:** present-set changes (U8) → past-set changes (U9) → future will/shall (U10) → reporting questions (U11) → reporting requests/commands and exclamatory sentences (U12), plus revision in Review-2.
- **Clause-level grammar arrives late and fast:** phrase vs clause and adverb phrase/clause (U6), conjunction families including correlatives (U7), compound prepositions, prepositional phrases, gerunds/participles (U8), noun phrase/noun clause (U9), adjective phrase/adjective clause — i.e. relative clauses under that name (U11), simple/compound/complex sentences (U12).
- **Word-level work recycles the earlier grades at a higher density:** dictionary anatomy (U1), thesaurus, silent letters, diphthongs/triphthongs, plural rules, countable/uncountable (U2), pronoun case/reflexive/antecedent/indefinite (U3), compound words, degrees of adjectives, adjective formation, articles, apostrophe (U4), idioms/proverbs, sentence kinds, homographs, homophones (U5), abbreviations/acronyms, adverbs (U6), capitonyms, denotation/connotation (U7), synonyms/antonyms (U9), antonyms and verb types (U10), transitive/intransitive (U11).
- **Writing progression:** mind-mapped paragraph and narrative (U1) → story with a moral (U2) → paraphrase and summary (U3) → newspaper report (U4) → summary and essay (U5, U6) → poem summary and own poem (U7) → story with title (U8) → descriptive essay with sensory language (U9) → recipe, essay, informal letter (U10) → magazine article and poem (U11) → diary page, email and formal-letter analysis (U12). Proofreading/editing with a peer-review checklist recurs in U1, U5, U9, U11 and U12.

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 129 PDF pages (printed pp.1–122; the missing Review-03 is noted separately and cannot be checked):

- **Passive voice:** never taught. There is no active/passive distinction, formation or exercise anywhere. Passive sentences occur incidentally in reading texts and in one present-perfect example (“The seats of the bus have already been sold”, printed p.54) without comment.
- **Conditionals:** no conditional type (zero, first, second, third, mixed) is taught or named. *If…then* appears only as a cause-and-effect signal phrase (p.81) and inside ordinary sentences.
- **Future perfect and future perfect continuous:** never presented. U7 teaches only simple future and future continuous under the heading “Future Tense”; the future perfect is not named anywhere in the book.
- **Relative clauses under that name, and relative pronouns as a topic:** not taught as such. The same ground is covered as “adjective clause” in U11 (examples with *who, which, that*), but no relative-pronoun table or defining/non-defining distinction is given.
- **Modal verbs as a system:** only a single definition box plus five gap-fills (U7, p.75). No functions (ability, permission, obligation, advice…) are taught, unlike Grades 6 and 7, which give function tables.
- **Question tags, inversion, subjunctive, causative, reported speech for past-perfect/future-perfect originals beyond the U9 chart:** not taught.
- **Exam preparation:** none. No board-style or model paper, no marks scheme, no timed practice; Reviews 1 and 2 are ordinary mixed revision exercises in the units' own format.
- **Answer keys:** none. No exercise, review, conversion exercise or proofreading passage is supplied with corrected answers (the U4/U9 proofreading passages print the errors but not the corrected version).
- **Back matter:** no IPA chart, no consolidated word list, no index — pronunciation is handled inside U2 (diphthongs/triphthongs) and U3 (intonation) only.

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments only)

- Printed p.33 (U4 reading): “whether the number of accidents and causalities can be reduced” — *causalities* printed for *casualties*.
- Printed p.51 (U5 Reading Skills): the comprehension questions are printed twice on one page — section “B. Comprehension Questions” (items 1–6) is followed, after the theme diagram, by “A. Answer the following questions” repeating items 1–4; the section letters also run B → A → D on this page, with “C. Kinds of Sentences” not appearing until p.53.
- Printed p.54 (U5 grammar): under the heading “Present Perfect Continuous Tense”, the example list includes one non-continuous sentence — “d. I have not seen her since 2010.” (item e in that list, “It has been raining since morning.”, is continuous as it should be; “We have finished our work.” appears on the same page but as item e of the Present Perfect Tense list above. Wording corrected at the independent citation check, 2026-11-04.)
- Printed p.38–40 (U4 Vocabulary and Grammar): section lettering duplicates — “C. Degrees of Adjectives”, “D. Articles”, “E. Apostrophe”, then on p.40 a second “D. Tenses”.
- Printed p.79 (U8 glossary): “accidently — by accident” — the glossary headword is mis-spelt (for *accidentally*) while its own definition is spelled correctly.
- Printed p.63 (U6 vocabulary): the basketball paragraph to be completed says James Naismith's game “was called ‘Duck on a Flock’” — for the children's game “duck on a rock”.
- Printed p.104 (U10 Writing): the model letter is introduced as task “C. Letter to a friend…” although a “C. Proofreading and Editing” already appears in the same unit's Writing Skills on p.95, and the unit's grammar section C is Direct and Indirect Speech.
- Printed p.122 (U12 Writing): the rubric says “Read the given letter to the Director of Education regarding the shortage of textbooks”, but the letter printed below it is addressed to “The Managing Director, Punjab Curriculum & Textbook Board, Lahore.”
- Printed p.106 (U11 facts box): “Largest Lake: Manchar Lake” is printed in a list that also gives “Longest River: The Indus River” — the lake is normally spelled Manchar/Manchhar; the spelling here is as printed and is left uncorrected in the book.
- Printed p.47–48 (U5 Talk Time): the poem “Prayer” is split across the unit opener and the next page, so its second half (“Who earn Your anger…”) appears *after* the “About the poet” box and the Pre-reading questions for the Hazrat Umar text, with no repeated title.

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **The file is truncated at the end — the most serious defect in the three-book set.** The PDF's last page is printed p.122, which ends Unit 12's Writing Skills (formal-letter text plus a proofreading checklist). The Content List (printed p.iii) promises **“Review - 03 … 123”**, and there is **no Review-03, no closing page and no back cover** in this file. At least printed p.123 onwards is missing; how many pages Review-03 ran to cannot be determined from this copy.
- **Watermark:** every content page carries a large diagonal watermark, “Web Version of PCTB Textbook / Not for Sale”, crossing text, tables and photographs. This is a property of the web-version source, present throughout.
- **Folio continuity (for what is present):** checked page by page — printed folios run 1–122 in unbroken sequence with no missing, duplicated or misbound pages inside that range, and unit openers fall where the Content List puts them (U1 p.1, U2 p.10, U3 p.22, U4 p.31, Review-01 p.42, U5 p.47, U6 p.57, U7 p.68, U8 p.76, Review-02 p.85, U9 p.88, U10 p.96, U11 p.105, U12 p.114). The roman-numeral front matter (ii, iii, iv, v, vi) is likewise complete.
- **Other marks:** no library stamps or handwriting; several pages are mostly white by design (e.g. printed p.95, whose lower two-thirds are blank). The spelling/typing errors listed in §6 are in the printed book itself (several are deliberate, in the proofreading exercises on pp.37 and 94, where corrupted versions of the units' own reading texts are set to be corrected).

## Pakistan — English, Grade 9 (file: g09.pdf)

Reader draft from a whole-book, page-by-page read. Page numbers below are PRINTED page numbers unless marked "PDF p." Summaries are my own words; quoted fragments are short headings/lines only.

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed (cover):** "ENGLISH 9"
- **Publisher/board as printed (cover):** "PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE"
- **Curriculum basis as printed (cover):** "Based on Updated/Revised National Curriculum of Pakistan 2023"
- **Curriculum basis as printed (imprint, PDF p.4):** "This textbook is based on Updated/Revised National Curriculum of Pakistan 2023 and has been approved by the Board."
- **Edition as printed (imprint):** "Experimental Edition"; the printing table gives Edition "Experimental", Impression "1st". The Date of Printing, Copies and Price cells are BLANK, and the "Published by:" / "Printed by:" lines are BLANK — no printer, no print date, no price is printed in this copy.
- **ISBN:** none printed anywhere in the book (checked cover, imprint page, back pages).
- **Year:** no year is printed on cover or imprint. (PDF file metadata, not printed: created Dec 2024.)
- **Authors as printed (imprint):** Ms. Ruhi Zaka Malik, Ms. Fatima Dar, Ms. Zarqa Bashir; Editor: Mr. Safdir Hussain; "(Authors) Aligned with Revised/Updated NCP-2023 by" Ms. Aamna Aamad, Mr. Malik Karrar Hussain Jakhar, Ms. Shazia Manzoor, Ms. Rubia Younas, Mr. Safdir Hussain; ERC Panels 1–2 and board officers are also named; Design & Layout: Ms. Minal Tariq.
- **PDF page count:** 168 pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** printed page = PDF page − 4. Verified on foliated pages across the book (e.g. PDF p.20 carries printed folio 16; PDF p.168 carries printed folio 164). Printed pagination runs 1–164; PDF pp.1–4 are cover, contents (2 pp.) and imprint.
- **Copy feature (cover and throughout):** a large diagonal stamp across the cover and repeated on interior pages reads "NOT FOR SALE PCTB" (see §7).

## 2. Architecture

Eleven units plus two review sections. Every unit follows the same skeleton: Learning Outcomes list → pre-reading activity → main text (prose passage, poem, or play) with while-reading margin questions → Theme note → Glossary table → "Reading and Critical Thinking" (open questions + MCQs + skills tasks) → Vocabulary work → Grammar section → Oral Communication Skills → Writing Skills → "For the Teacher" guidance boxes throughout. The two-page contents table (PDF pp.2–3) maps each unit's theme, reading focus, vocabulary/grammar, oral and writing strands, and start page.

Unit map (printed start pages, as listed in the contents and verified at each unit heading):

| Unit | Title | Printed start |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The Saviour of Mankind | 1 |
| 2 | Patriotism | 16 |
| 3 | Daffodils (Poem) | 31 |
| 4 | Hazrat Asma (RA) | 49 |
| 5 | Women Empowerment Through Entrepreneurship | 58 |
| — | Review 1 (Units 1–6) | 83 |
| 7 | If (Poem) | 86 |
| 8 | The Impact of Globalisation on Culture and Economy | 93 |
| 9 | Quality Education: A Key to Success | 106 |
| 10 | The Silent Predator and the Majestic Prey – Snow Leopard and Markhor | 120 |
| 11 | The Dear Departed (one-act play) | 132 |
| — | Review 2 (Units 7–11) | 160 |

(Numbering note: the poem "If" is Unit 7; there is no separately numbered Unit 6 heading gap — "The Value of Time" is Unit 6, printed p.72. Full order: 1 Saviour, 2 Patriotism, 3 Daffodils, 4 Hazrat Asma, 5 Women Empowerment, 6 Value of Time, Review 1, 7 If, 8 Globalisation, 9 Quality Education, 10 Snow Leopard/Markhor, 11 Dear Departed, Review 2.)

## 3. Unit-by-unit

### Unit 1 — The Saviour of Mankind (printed pp.1–15) [read from page images — this unit has no text layer in this PDF; see §7]
- **Text (pp.1–5):** expository prose life-sketch of the Prophet Muhammad (PBUH) (theme: Ethics and Values / Justice, Handling of Trust / Self-discipline). Summary: Arabia before Islam and the Arabs' famed memory and eloquence (the Ukaz poetry fair; Hammad's boast to Caliph Walid bin Yazid); a world in 5th–6th-century chaos; the Prophet's retreats to Cave Hira and the first revelation (Quran 96:1–5 quoted in translation); twenty-three years of revelation; pagan opposition and the delegation to his uncle Abu Talib, answered with the "sun in my right hand and the moon in my left" resolve; a long supporting quotation from Michael H. Hart's *The 100* ranking him the most influential person in history; Hazrat Ayesha's description of his character as an embodiment of the Quran; closing verses Quran 33:45–46. A Theme box and a two-page glossary follow (bestow, compassion, crumbling, dazzling, dilemma, dispensation, dune, eloquence, embodiment, eradicate, meditation, nexus, proclaim, promulgation, proselytizing, renounce, tropical, unparalleled…).
- **Reading work (pp.6–7):** 8 analytical comprehension questions (geography of Makkah, the Hammad anecdote, parallels with other ages of decline/renewal, evidence-based inference about the author's view of pre-Islamic Arabs); identify the main idea and supporting details of paragraph 5; write an objective summary of the text.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.7–13):** the book's foundation language block — (a) a full IPA pronunciation key page (British RP given first, American variants noted; consonant, vowel and diphthong charts); (b) dictionary skills taught over a reproduced dictionary page (guide words, entry word, pronunciation, part of speech, definition; etymology in the teacher notes), with an exercise looking up imagination / remarkable / composed / preservation and comparing dictionary meanings with inferences; (c) word patterns — word families (analyze → analysis → analytical → analytically), prefixes (re-), suffixes (-er), an "advocate" word-family chart exercise; (d) **parts of speech** taught in full: noun, pronoun (personal, possessive, reflexive, demonstrative, interrogative and relative pronouns listed as types), verb (action/linking/helping), adjective (qualitative, quantitative, demonstrative, possessive), adverb (how/when/where/extent), preposition, conjunction (coordinating, subordinating, correlative), interjection — each with a definition and example sentences, closing with labelling, sentence-building and MCQ exercises (pp.12–13).
- **Oral (pp.13–14):** group oral discussion of a text section (main idea + supporting details, group presentations); respond by telling short stories (retell the first revelation in own words; tell a story showing the Prophet's compassion or determination); attentive-listening work — a passage on a favourite personality read aloud, questions on the reader's emotion and pronunciation, paired read-aloud with peer feedback.
- **Writing (pp.14–15):** process approach to paragraph writing (brainstorm → organise ideas into main idea + supporting details → mind map → summarise key points), worked on the topic "Tolerance and Religious Harmony" with a model structure (main idea: respecting self and others…) and brainstorming examples printed; then write an informative essay on "Peace and Tolerance".

### Unit 2 — Patriotism (printed pp.16–30)
- **Text (pp.18–19):** expository prose in 5 numbered paragraphs defining patriotism, its Latin origin, the supreme values of a patriot (sovereignty, integrity, honour), Quaid-e-Azam Muhammad Ali Jinnah as nation-builder, the wars of 1965, 1971 and Kargil 1999 and the Nishan-e-Haider recipients named in a roll, closing with the S. W. Scott verse lines. Theme box and glossary (commendable, devoid, devotion, embrace, martyrdom, sovereignty…).
- **Reading work (pp.20–23):** 8 open questions; 5 MCQs; cause-and-effect identification; fact vs opinion; generalized statement vs evidence-based information; main-idea work on paragraph 2.
- **Vocabulary (pp.23–24):** inferred meanings from context clues; dictionary work (pronunciation, meaning, part of speech, etymology) for commendable/render/sacrifice/sovereignty; word-pattern family of "sovereignty".
- **Grammar (pp.24–29):** a full 12-tense table (simple/continuous/perfect/perfect-continuous × present/past/future) with function, example and exercise per tense; tense-identification passage and timeline task; then **Gerunds**, **Infinitives**, **Gerund vs Infinitive** meaning contrasts (remember/stop/try/forget/regret/go on), and **Participles** (present/past, as adjectives, in tenses, in the passive, in participial phrases) with labelling and transformation exercises.
- **Oral (p.30):** group discussion of text sections; debate on "The Role of Youth in the Nation-Building".
- **Writing (p.30):** paragraph on "My Service to Society"; informative/explanatory essay "I Love Pakistan".

### Unit 3 — Daffodils (printed pp.31–48)
- **Text (pp.32–33):** Wordsworth's poem (all four stanzas) with while-reading questions; "About the Poem and the Poet" note (Ullswater walk, 15 April 1802, with Dorothy); theme analysis listing four themes plus a list of other Wordsworth poems to read.
- **Reading work (pp.34–35):** 8 open questions (central idea, setting, personification, rhyme scheme effect…); 10 MCQs.
- **Vocabulary (pp.36–37):** denotation vs connotation vs nuance, worked through lonely/solitary; word relationships (synonyms, antonyms, homophones, homonyms, analogies).
- **Grammar (pp.38–44):** figurative language taught from the poem — simile, personification, hyperbole, metaphor, alliteration, assonance, imagery — each defined with a poem example; then **Euphemism and Oxymoron** (pp.41–42), explicitly noting the poem contains neither; then **Compound Prepositions** (according to, due to, in front of, on behalf of, in addition to, in spite of…) and **Prepositional Phrases** (time/location/direction/cause/manner/condition; adjective vs adverb phrases; common errors).
- **Oral (p.45):** recitation/discussion of an extract of Taufiq Rafat's "Arrival of the Monsoon" (last six lines printed).
- **Writing (pp.46–48):** paraphrasing (worked paraphrase of stanza 1; paraphrase the last stanza); summary writing in four steps (reading, outlining, drafting, reviewing) and an objective summary of "Daffodils".

### Unit 4 — Hazrat Asma (RA) (printed pp.49–57) [read from page images — no text layer in this PDF; see §7]
- **Text (pp.50–52):** narrative account of Hazrat Asma (RA), daughter of Hazrat Abu Bakr Siddique (RA), during the Hijrah of 622 CE (theme: Handling of Trust / Self-discipline / Respecting Self and Others). Summary: the Quraysh offer rewards for the Prophet (PBUH) and Abu Bakr; at Abu Bakr's house Asma prepares the journey food and, finding nothing else, ties it on the camel with her own belt — earning her the title "Zaat-un-Nitaqain" (She of the Two Belts); nightly she carries food through the mountains to the Cave of Thawr; Abu Jehl storms the house demanding her father's whereabouts, slaps her so hard her earring falls off, and she still keeps the secret; for her blind grandfather Abu Quhafa, fearing Abu Bakr has taken all the wealth, she puts pebbles under a cloth where the money was kept so he feels "gold and jewels" and is comforted; she was among the earliest Muslims, wife of Zubair bin al-Awwam, mother of Abdullah bin Zubair, famed for generosity (she sold her inherited garden and gave the money to the poor), and died aged about a hundred. Theme box and glossary (alleviate, incessant, furious, migrate, render, resolute…) follow.
- **Reading work (p.53–54):** 7 analytical questions (resourcefulness and courage, trust and integrity today, a counterfactual on the secret); 5 MCQs on handling of trust, self-discipline, respect and justice in her actions.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.54–56):** inferred meaning — deduce word meanings from context clues (delicate, endangered, rugged…); dictionary work including etymology (advocacy, catalyst, empowerment, resilience, substantial) and word-pattern charts (bounty, determine, infuriate, prepare, reward); then **application, not new teaching**: underline the verbs in a passage about the unit and name their tenses; rewrite the passage in the simple present; write a paragraph using at least Simple Past, Past Perfect and Past Continuous; identify gerunds/infinitives/participles in unit-based sentences and complete sentences with the correct -ing/to-form (this unit re-uses Unit 2's tense and verb-form teaching, as its learning outcomes promise — "use all types of tenses correctly… use gerunds, infinitives, and participles").
- **Oral (p.56):** role-play interview — a journalist interviews a student playing an Islamic scholar on Asma's contributions and their modern parallels; group discussion on "Handling of Trust".
- **Writing (p.57):** narrative writing taught as a five-step process (planning → writing → revising → editing → finalizing): write a narrative of a pivotal moment of Asma's courage during the migration, using dialogue, pacing and detailed description, closing with a reflection on trust and responsibility.

### Unit 5 — Women Empowerment Through Entrepreneurship (printed pp.58–71) [read from page images — no text layer in this PDF; see §7]
- **Text (pp.58–62):** expository essay (theme: Entrepreneurship / Success Stories of Entrepreneurs / Women Empowerment). Summary: what entrepreneurship is; why it empowers women beyond income; Hazrat Khadija (RA) as the earliest great example — a wealthy Makkah businesswoman whose trading success in a male-dominated society still inspires; modern Pakistani women entrepreneurs in cities and villages; support structures (microfinance loans, mentorship programmes, women's chambers of commerce); the five barriers — cultural expectations, limited access to resources, mobility issues, legal and institutional barriers, lack of support networks; the sectors women work in (fashion and textiles, food and catering, handicrafts, education and training centres, e-commerce and tech startups); the benefits (economic growth, job creation, role models for future generations, community development, gender equality, innovation); government and NGO support. Theme box and glossary (corporation, entrepreneurship, innovative, microfinance, norm, societal, stereotype, strategic, thrive…) follow.
- **Reading work (pp.63–65):** 8 analytical questions; 5 MCQs; a cloze completion drawn from the text.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.65–68):** denotative vs connotative meanings of entrepreneur / empowerment / independence / barrier from context clues; **proverbs** (definition, "A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush" worked example, five proverbs to place in sentence frames); **idioms** (definition, matching exercise — break the ice, once in a blue moon, a piece of cake, hit the nail on the head, cost an arm and a leg — then idioms/proverbs spotted inside the unit's own text, e.g. "the odds stacked against them", with literal and figurative meanings); **punctuation and capitalisation** taught mark by mark (full stop, comma, apostrophe for possession and contraction, question mark, exclamation mark, capital letters), a punctuate-the-passage exercise on Fatima Jinnah, and a paragraph on "The Role of Women Empowerment in the Society" written to stated punctuation/capitalisation/spelling/hyphenation standards.
- **Oral (pp.68–70):** group discussion of text sections with findings presented to the class; research-and-tell short stories of women entrepreneurs with role-play; a scripted **community-meeting role-play** (woman entrepreneur, community members, local government official, aspiring entrepreneur — outline and sample dialogue lines printed); discussion on handling challenges (resilience, community-focused entrepreneurship); a video task — watch the printed YouTube-short link and complete a name/achievement table of women pioneers (Hazrat Khadija, Maryam Al-Astrulabi, Sutayta Al-Mahamali and others); favourite-entrepreneur discussion.
- **Writing (pp.70–71):** the draft–edit–proofread process taught in three parts: Part 1 write a first draft (a paragraph on the impact of a woman entrepreneur, as biography or autobiography); Part 2 edit the draft for content, clarity and coherence against printed editing questions; Part 3 proofread against a printed checklist (complete sentences, punctuation, spelling, capitalisation); finally write a formal application to a business organisation requesting sponsorship for a women's-entrepreneurs' workshop, with the letter's parts (subject, salutation, complimentary closure, signature) labelled in a format diagram.

### Unit 6 — The Value of Time (printed pp.72–82)
- **Text (pp.73–75):** original short story in 13 numbered paragraphs: Taseer, cheerful but a chronic procrastinator, is taught prioritising by his grandfather with the jar/rocks/pebbles/sand demonstration; he reforms, improves at school, and retells the lesson. Theme box on character viewpoints; glossary (cherished, elucidate, gratification, intrigued, methodically, perpetually…).
- **Reading work (pp.76–77):** 5 open questions; a full plot-development review — exposition, rising action, climax, falling action, resolution — with a plot diagram to complete.
- **Vocabulary (p.78):** meanings of highlighted words; thesaurus synonyms (engrossed, admirable, frowned, amazement, allocated, impact, cherished); noun→adjective changes (nature→natural…).
- **Grammar (pp.78–81):** **Conjunctions** — co-ordinating (FANBOYS), subordinating, correlative — with fill-in exercises; **Transition words/devices** with examples and sentence-writing.
- **Oral (p.81):** group discussion questions on the story's counterfactuals and the big-rocks analogy.
- **Writing (pp.81–82):** elements of a story (setting, characters, plot, conflict, resolution, theme) and a step-by-step story-writing task (write a story of an unexpected friendship), with draft review checklist.

### Review 1 — Units 1–6 (printed pp.83–85) [read from page images — no text layer in this PDF; see §7]
- **Reading (p.83):** 5 questions spanning Units 1–5 (the author's view of pre-Islamic Arabs; patriotism in disasters and pandemics; the daffodils' effect on the poet then and in recollection; Hazrat Asma's contribution and a counterfactual; entrepreneurship's influence beyond economics).
- **Vocabulary (p.83):** dictionary table for solitude / devotion / jocund / rapture / innovative (pronunciation, meaning, part of speech); explain highlighted words in sentences from the Unit 6 story (procrastinating, impart, eagerly, intently…).
- **Grammar (pp.83–84):** fill blanks with the part of speech named in brackets (5 items); name the tense of 5 sentences; sort underlined forms into gerund/infinitive/participle (5 items); rewrite 5 sentences with correct punctuation and capitalisation; write a paragraph using at least three transitional words.
- **Oral (pp.84–85):** a scripted role-play — a school assembly on self-discipline with assigned roles (School Counsellor, Student, School Principal, Class Representative), dialogue preparation guidance and performance steps; a debate on "The Role of Youth in the Nation-Building" with attentive-listening and pronunciation requirements.
- **Writing (p.85):** a reflective response on respecting someone very different from oneself; a narrative about a character's journey to fight for justice in their community.

### Unit 7 — If (printed pp.86–92)
- **Text (p.87):** Kipling's poem "If—" complete (four stanzas), with poet note (Rudyard Kipling 1865–1936). Glossary (imposter, knave, sinew, stoop, triumph, virtue).
- **Reading work (p.88):** 7 open questions on the poem's advice (criticism, self-trust, dreams, loss, resilience, integrity).
- **Vocabulary (pp.88–89):** contextual clues explained and practised on the poem; identifying figures of speech in given sentences.
- **Grammar (p.89):** conjunctions and transitional devices — fill-ins and sentence-rewriting (revision of Unit 6 grammar in this unit's context).
- **Oral (pp.89–91):** group discussion on "The Impact of Excessive Screen Time on Mental Health"; a model dialogue (Kiran/Zaaraa on joining a gardening club) for pair practice; compose and recite an own poem.
- **Writing (pp.91–92):** paraphrasing steps and paraphrase of stanzas 2–3 of "If"; summary-writing rules with a worked summary of Frost's "Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening" (summary only — the Frost poem itself is not printed), then write a summary of "If".

### Unit 8 — The Impact of Globalisation on Culture and Economy (printed pp.93–105)
- **Text (pp.93–96):** expository essay in 15 numbered paragraphs: definition and history of globalisation; trade/investment effects on Pakistan (textiles, rice, sports goods; CPEC named); multinational jobs; skilled labour; digital globalisation, social media, cultural exchange vs erosion of traditional culture; balancing conclusion. Theme box; large glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.97–98):** 8 open questions; 7 MCQs.
- **Vocabulary (p.99):** anagrams (worked example "vulnerable"); make words from globalisation/integration/information/homogenisation/communication; glossary words in own sentences.
- **Grammar (pp.99–101):** sentence structure — simple sentences (subject/verb/object), then **complex sentences** (independent + dependent clauses, subordinating conjunctions), identification and own-writing tasks. (No compound-sentence or compound-complex treatment.)
- **Oral (pp.101–102):** mind-map question-and-answer oral practice (Aim in Life / Myself / Family / Favourite Game / Hobby / School); paired reflection on digital globalisation.
- **Writing (pp.102–105):** précis writing — rules (read, identify main points, condense to own words, third person, revise), a worked example (science passage with its précis and title "Benefits of Science"), then write a précis at one-third length with title for four passages (renewable energy; nature; education; healthy eating).

### Unit 9 — Quality Education: A Key to Success (printed pp.106–119)
- **Text (pp.106–110):** essay in 14 numbered paragraphs on quality education and SDG 4: economic growth, empowerment, human capital, skills (literacy/numeracy/digital/vocational), inequality and poverty, social cohesion and peace, innovation and STEM, health, and the challenges (outdated curricula, teacher shortage/low pay/training). Theme box; glossary (bedrock, cohesion, formidable, holistic, pedagogical…).
- **Reading work (pp.111–114):** 8 open questions; 8 MCQs; an SDG-icons task (identify two goals besides Goal 4 that support education quality); analysis of a printed eyewitness account of education reforms (Government Primary School, Lahore, dated 12th March 2024) — facts, tone, personal perspective; highlight impactful words in paragraphs 1, 2, 3, 10.
- **Vocabulary (p.114):** contextual clues in a Karachi street passage (bustling, aromatic, vibrant, resilience, serene, energy, hustle) with synonym work and fill-ins.
- **Grammar (pp.115–116):** **Phrases** — adjectival, prepositional and adverbial phrases defined and identified in a passage; sentence-expansion exercises.
- **Oral (pp.116–117):** role-play script printed in full, "The Power of Education" (3 scenes: The Struggle to Learn / The Call for Change / The Impact of Quality Education).
- **Writing (pp.117–119):** book review — purpose, 7 components (introduction, plot summary, character analysis, themes, writing style, personal opinion, conclusion/recommendation), a guided 200–300-word review task on "Little Women" by Louisa May Alcott (retold by John Escott), then a review of any book of choice.

### Unit 10 — The Silent Predator and the Majestic Prey – Snow Leopard and Markhor (printed pp.120–131)
- **Text (pp.120–123):** descriptive/informative text in 14 numbered paragraphs: snow leopard ("Ghost of the Mountains", up to 5,500 m) and markhor (Pakistan's national animal, horns to 1.5 m, name from Persian "snake-eater"), predator–prey balance, threats (poaching, habitat destruction, climate change), conservation (Snow Leopard Foundation, WWF-Pakistan, Chitral National Park / Toshi Game Reserve trophy-hunting programmes, herder compensation), ecosystem role. Theme box; glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.124–125):** 8 open questions; 5 MCQs; a cause/problem/analyse/solution/effect graphic-organiser task on a short conservation text.
- **Vocabulary (p.126):** denotative vs connotative meanings of balance/elusive/heritage/predator/vulnerable from context clues.
- **Grammar (pp.126–127):** hyphenated nouns and adjectives (mother-in-law, well-known; prefixes self-/ex-/co-/non-/anti-); words with the letter string **-ough** and its pronunciations (though/through/rough/enough/thought/dough/bough/cough) with exercises.
- **Oral (pp.127–129):** group section discussion with assigned roles; storytelling about a conservation organisation; role-play of a community conservation meeting; class debate (saving the snow leopard vs the markhor); attentive-listening pronunciation practice.
- **Writing (pp.129–131):** descriptive composition on the snow leopard or markhor, general→specific, via brainstorming, mind mapping and first draft, with punctuation/spelling checks.

### Unit 11 — The Dear Departed (one-act play) (printed pp.132–159)
- **Pre-matter (pp.132–134):** character list with relationships (Mrs. Amelia Slater, Henry Slater, Mrs. Elizabeth Jordan, Ben Jordan, Victoria Slater, Mr. Abel Merryweather); a prose description of the Edwardian living-room set; a textbook section defining the one-act play (single act, focused plot, limited characters, single setting, theme-centred) and its purposes.
- **Play text (pp.135–150):** Stanley Houghton's one-act play printed in full. Plot summary: the Slaters, believing Abel Merryweather dead upstairs, move his bureau and clock downstairs before the Jordans arrive to divide his things; the family squabbles over mourning, the obituary verse and his insurance premium; Victoria discovers Abel alive; Abel exposes the "robbery", announces a new will favouring whoever he lives with when he dies, and then announces he will marry Mrs. John Shorrocks of the 'Ring-o'-Bells' on Monday — curtain.
- **Post-matter (p.150):** "Background of the Play" note (written 1908, Manchester School, Edwardian materialism) and playwright biography (William Stanley Houghton 1881–1913; Hindle Wakes 1912 named); theme box (materialism and hypocrisy). Glossary (bureau, gallivanting, hearth, impassive, malicious, piqued, precocious, shabby, speck, stupefied).
- **Reading work (pp.151–153):** 10 open questions (motivations, satire, hypocrisy, Victoria's innocence, the clock as symbol, single-room setting…); 5 MCQs; plot-diagram discussion using the Unit 6 diagram.
- **Vocabulary (pp.153–154):** denotative/connotative table; antonym MCQs (shabby, impassive, delicate, precocious, heartiness); word-building from root words (colour, count, play, work, faith; model: Aud).
- **Grammar (pp.154–155):** **Direct and Indirect Narration** — definitions and worked conversions; 17 sentences to convert (statements, questions, commands, requests, exclamations, universal truth) plus reverse items given in indirect form; a passage to change into direct speech. (This is the book's only reported-speech teaching.)
- **Oral (pp.156–157):** group discussion of play sections; research-and-narrate task on Houghton; perform scenes; enact the whole play; discuss humour.
- **Writing (pp.157–159):** play-writing mechanics — analyse a (newly written, not-from-the-play) model scene, then write an own short scene and a multi-character dialogue (15–20 lines) with director's notes/background narration in brackets, with a conventions checklist and peer review.

### Review 2 — Units 7–11 (printed pp.160–164)
- **Reading (p.160):** 5 questions (advice in "If" today; globalisation and cultural spread; snow leopard/markhor problems and solutions; the irony of the title "The Dear Departed"; humour in the play).
- **Vocabulary (pp.160–161):** dictionary information table (globalisation, crucial, population, sustainable, departed); pairs of words in sentences (compliment/complement, eligible/illegible, foul/fowl, gentle/genteel, respectable/respectful, wander/wonder, yoke/yolk); root-word building repeated from Unit 11.
- **Grammar (p.161):** conjunction fill-ins; simple vs complex sentence classification with reasons; 5 direct→indirect speech conversions.
- **Oral (pp.162–163):** paired direct/indirect speech conversion role-play (teacher–student); one-minute speeches on the 17 SDGs; group wildlife-experience storytelling; group discussion of the play's theme.
- **Writing (pp.163–164):** paraphrase the opening lines of "If"; descriptive composition on quality education (brainstorm → mind map → draft, general to specific); paragraph on "My Service to Society".

## 4. Grammar / vocabulary progression summary

- **Tense system:** taught once, completely, in Unit 2 — all 12 tenses in one table with functions and examples — and then only revisited inside other topics (participles "to form perfect tenses", narration changes in Unit 11). No later unit re-teaches or extends tenses.
- **Verb forms:** gerunds, infinitives, gerund-vs-infinitive meaning changes, and participles are all taught in Unit 2; Unit 4 then re-applies the same tense/verb-form material in exercises on its own text (identify tenses, rewrite in the simple present, sort gerunds/infinitives/participles), exactly as its learning outcomes promise.
- **Sentence level:** conjunctions + transition words (Unit 6, revised Unit 7), simple vs complex sentences (Unit 8), phrases — adjectival/prepositional/adverbial (Unit 9; compound prepositions and prepositional phrases earlier, Unit 3).
- **Reported speech:** one section only, Unit 11 (direct/indirect narration incl. questions, commands, requests, exclamations), practised again in Review 2.
- **Word study strand (one per unit):** dictionary skills and word patterns (U1); context inference + dictionary + etymology (U2); denotation/connotation/nuance (U3, again U10); idioms and proverbs + punctuation (U5, per contents); thesaurus synonyms and noun→adjective derivation (U6); contextual clues (U7, U9); anagrams (U8); spelling, pairs of words, hyphens and -ough pronunciations (U10); root-word building and antonyms (U11).
- **Writing strand progression:** paragraph + informative essay (U1–U2) → paraphrase and summary (U3, U7) → narrative (U4) → drafting/editing/proofreading (U5) → story writing (U6) → précis (U8) → book review (U9) → descriptive composition (U10) → playscript/dialogue writing (U11), with both reviews re-testing the same skills.

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 168 PDF pages:
- **Passive voice:** never taught as a topic. It is named once, inside the participles section (printed p.29: past participles "To form the passive voice" with one example sentence), with no formation rules, no tense-by-tense passive, and no active↔passive transformation exercises anywhere.
- **Conditionals:** no conditional types are taught — no zero/first/second/third conditional section, no if-clause tense patterns. "If" appears only as the Kipling poem and as an ordinary subordinating conjunction in lists.
- **Reported speech:** present (Unit 11 only, see above) — this is the exception, not a gap.
- **Relative clauses:** never taught — no defining/non-defining relative clauses and no clause-building work. Relative pronouns (who, whom, whose, which, that) appear only as one line in Unit 1's list of pronoun types (printed p.11), with no clause teaching attached.
- **Other absent grammar:** no articles (a/an/the) section; no modal verbs section; no subject–verb agreement section (named only inside Unit 8 learning outcomes as a proofreading target); no question-formation/negatives section; no degrees of comparison; no direct treatment of compound sentences (only simple and complex are defined).
- **Examination preparation:** none. There are no past papers, no model/board-style questions, no pairing scheme, no marking scheme, and no exam strategy anywhere in the book. The two Review sections are ordinary revision exercises, not exam-format practice.
- **Answer keys:** none. No exercise in the book is answered in the book (the worked examples inside grammar explanations are the only models given; even the Review sections carry no key).

## Pakistan — English Book I, Grade 11 — Short Stories (file: g11.pdf)

Reader draft from a whole-book, page-by-page read. Page numbers below are PRINTED page numbers unless marked "PDF p." Summaries are my own words; quoted fragments are short headings/lines only. For the stories only plot/theme summaries are given, never the text.

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed (cover):** "ENGLISH BOOK I" with a class badge "11" and the session "2018-19". **Title page (PDF p.3):** "ENGLISH BOOK – I / FOR / INTERMEDIATE CLASSES / (Short Stories)"
- **Publisher/board as printed (title page):** "PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE"
- **Curriculum basis as printed (imprint, PDF p.4):** "Prepared and published vide NOC No. F7- 8/2003-English, Government of Pakistan, Ministry of Education (Curriculum Wing), Islamabad." No named national-curriculum statement (of the 2023 kind) appears — this is the older PCTB intermediate book.
- **Compilers as printed:** Bashir Ahmed Chaudhry; Qazi Sajjad Ahmed. Editor: Mrs. Shahida Rasul. Director (Manuscripts): Dr. Mobeen Akhtar. Supervised by: Safdir Hussain. Layout: Hafiz Inam-ul-Haq.
- **Year/edition as printed (imprint):** "DATE OF PRINTING: APRIL 2018; EDITION: 1ST; IMPRESSION: 20TH; NO OF COPIES: 18,000; PRICE: 41/-". Published by: Taxation Law Book Publishers, Lahore; Printed by: Qudrat Ullah Printers, Lahore. **No ISBN is printed** anywhere in the front matter of this copy.
- **Front matter (PDF p.2):** a Quaid-e-Azam Muhammad Ali Jinnah quotation on education (Karachi, September 26, 1947), the national anthem in Urdu, and the PCTB security-sticker notice (the rectangular hologram sticker on the title page, verifiable by SMS to 8070).
- **PDF page count:** 104 pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** printed page = PDF page − 4. Verified on foliated pages (PDF p.10 carries the running head "Short Stories" and printed folio 6; PDF p.5 = printed p.1, the first story page). Printed pagination runs 1–100; PDF pp.1–4 are cover, Quaid/anthem/sticker page, title page and contents/imprint page.

## 2. Architecture

A pure literature anthology: **15 short stories ("Lessons")** and nothing else — no units, no skills strands, no separate grammar course. Every lesson follows the same apparatus: the story printed in full → a short **Theme** paragraph → **Reading Notes** (a glossary of hard words and phrases from the story) → **EXERCISES**: (1) choose the correct answer (MCQs), (2) true/false statements, (3) short answer questions, (4) long answer questions in 100–150 words, and (5) rotating language exercises (sentence matching, verb-form completion, punctuation, indirect narration, and similar — varying by lesson). Contents (PDF p.4):

| Lesson | Title | Printed start |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Button, Button | 1 |
| 2 | Clearing in the Sky | 11 |
