## 3. Lesson-by-lesson

### Lesson 1 — Button, Button, Richard Matheson (printed pp.1–10)
- **Story (pp.1–7):** a stranger, Mr. Steward, leaves Norma and Arthur Lewis a button unit: press it and an unknown person somewhere in the world dies, and they receive $50,000. Arthur refuses — killing a stranger is still murder. Norma, dreaming of a Europe trip, a cottage and nicer things, persuades herself it must be a research experiment and presses the button while Arthur is at work; the call that comes is from Lenox Hill Hospital — Arthur has been pushed in front of a subway train. The box, smashed open, is empty; Mr. Steward's last line asks whether she really knew her husband. (Arthur's $25,000 life-insurance policy, double for accidental death, is the story's bitter arithmetic.)
- **Apparatus (pp.7–10):** Theme paragraph; Reading Notes glossary; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 8 short questions; 5 long questions (character of Arthur; why Norma did not remain true to her husband; whether killing an unseen person matters; the story in your own words; the moral lesson); a sentence-matching item; **language exercises: correct forms of verbs in brackets (5 sentences taken from the story), punctuate two unpunctuated lines, and rewrite 4 direct-speech lines in indirect narration.**

### Lesson 2 — Clearing in the Sky, Jesse Stuart (printed pp.11–17)
- **Story (pp.11–14):** the narrator Jess visits his 70-year-old father, whose weak heart has earned him forty years of doctors' orders not to work. The old man leads him up the mountain — by paths he cut at different steepnesses to measure his own recovery — to show him a small clearing he has fenced and planted with potatoes, yams and tomatoes on the very slope he and his wife cleared as newly-weds, because crops in new ground taste better and because a man on borrowed time goes back to the places he loved. Theme paragraph (love of nature, strength of the human spirit) and Reading Notes (97 in the shade; a wisp of alfalfa; bluff; loam; whiff; yam; stalwart; the Good Book; bandanna; black gum; a mess of fox squirrels…) follow.
- **Exercises (pp.15–17):** 10 MCQs; true/false statements; [remaining exercise pages read from the page images; see §7 note on the copy].

### Lesson 3 — Dark They Were, and Golden-Eyed, Ray Bradbury (printed pp.18–25)
- **Story (pp.18–22):** the Bittering family flee a coming atomic war on Earth for Mars. Stranded when atom bombs destroy New York and all rockets, Harry Bittering fights the planet's pull — refusing Martian food, trying alone to build a rocket — while the air, water and food quietly remake the settlers: eyes turn golden, bodies darken and lengthen, Earth words give way to Martian ones. In the end the family abandons the Earth town for a marble villa in the hills; five years later a rescue rocket finds the town empty and friendly dark, golden-eyed "Martians" in the hills who know nothing of the Earth settlers.
- **Apparatus (pp.23–25):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 8 short questions; 5 long questions (100–150 words); verb forms (10); punctuate the lines.

### Lesson 4 — Thank You, M'am, Langston Hughes (printed pp.26–31)
- **Story (pp.26–28):** late at night a boy, Roger, tries to snatch the purse of Mrs. Luella Bates Washington Jones and falls flat; instead of calling the police she grips him by the shirt, drags him home, makes him wash his face, feeds him, tells him she too has done things she would not tell, and gives him ten dollars for the blue suede shoes he wanted — leaving him unable even to say thank you at her door.
- **Apparatus (pp.28–31):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 10 short questions; 3 long questions (100–150 words); verb forms (10); punctuate the lines; **use the prepositions in, across, off, up, around in your own sentences.**

### Lesson 5 — The Piece of String (printed pp.32–37)
- **Story (pp.32–34):** an adapted version of Guy de Maupassant's story with the names changed (the peasant is "Mr. Hubert", the harness-maker "Mr. Manana", the loser "Mr. James"). Hubert picks up a piece of string in the market place; accused before the mayor of taking a lost pocketbook of five hundred shillings, searched and discharged, he is never believed — even after another man returns the pocketbook — and dies protesting "A piece of string, a piece of string!" This printing adds an ending Maupassant did not write: a great flood spares Hubert's grave, whose stone reads that the flood proved his innocence.
- **Apparatus (pp.34–37):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 6 short questions; 5 long questions (100–150 words); **preposition fill-ins (on, to, of, at, from); punctuate the lines; use purchase / attentive / surprised / astounded / shook in sentences; a passage-comprehension exercise** (the drumbeat announcement reprinted, with 4 questions).

### Lesson 6 — The Reward, Lord Dunsany (printed pp.38–43)
- **Story (pp.38–40):** in a club debate on opportunity vs determination, Jorkens tells of Gorgios, who at sixteen fixed on becoming Court acrobat — a post that did not exist. He spent a lifetime in politics preaching the nation's glory until the post was created, and was inaugurated into it as a white-haired old man who could only bow: more than sixty years of sticking to one wild idea. An "About the Author" note on Lord Dunsany (1878–1957) is printed with this lesson.
- **Apparatus (pp.40–43):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 10 short questions (in one or two sentences); 5 long questions (50–100 words); sentence-connection matching; punctuate the lines; **verb forms inside conditional sentences taken from the story; phrasal verbs (get in / out / on / off) in own sentences; passage comprehension (4 questions including the main idea).**

### Lesson 7 — The Use of Force, William Carlos Williams (printed pp.44–50)
- **Story (pp.44–46):** a doctor is called to Mathilda, feverish for three days; suspecting diphtheria, he must see her throat. The child claws at his eyes, snaps the wooden tongue depressor between her molars and fights "with clenched teeth, desperately", until — furious himself — he forces a heavy silver spoon down her throat and finds both tonsils covered with membrane: she had hidden her sore throat for three days. An "About the Author" note on Williams (1883–1963), physician and poet, is printed.
- **Apparatus (pp.47–50):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 8 MCQs; 8 true/false; 10 short questions; 6 long questions (50–100 words); verb forms (10); punctuate the lines; use words in sentences; **fill-in-the-blanks with words/particles from the story; phrasal verbs (sit on / sit in / come from / come off); passage comprehension (4 questions including main idea).**

### Lesson 8 — The Gulistan of Sa'di (printed pp.51–56)
- **Text (pp.51–53):** three parables from Sa'di's Gulistan in translation, with an introduction (Emerson's and Edwin Arnold's praise of Sa'di): (I) a king's terrified slave is thrown overboard by a sergeant and hauled back — only tasting danger teaches the worth of the boat's safety; (II) Nushirvan makes the boy pay for the salt fetched for his deer roast — "the foundation of oppression was small in the world"; a king who eats one apple from a subject's garden will have the tree pulled down; (III) a dying king needs the bile of a boy of certain qualities; the parents consent for wealth, the Qazi decrees, but the boy's smiling appeal to Allah shames the king, who frees and rewards him — and recovers within a week. An "About the Author" note (Sa'di, 1184–1292) and a Theme on rulers' virtues follow.
- **Apparatus (pp.53–56):** Reading Notes; Exercises — 11 MCQs; 10 true/false; 6 short questions; 6 long questions (50–100 words); **fill in the blanks from a given word list; use words in sentences; phrasal verbs (hit by / hit upon / ask for / ask after); punctuate the lines; passage comprehension (5 questions).**

### Lesson 9 — The Foolish Quack, folk tale translated by Rev. Charles Swynnerton (printed pp.57–61)
- **Story (pp.57–58):** a bystander watches camel-men cure a camel with a melon stuck in its throat (blanket round the neck, one blow). Setting up as a doctor who "can cure the goitre", he treats an old woman the same way and kills her; beaten and made to dig her grave, he tries again in the next village — asking in advance not to be made to dig the grave — is driven off, and is finally taught by a camel-man's stick, struck on a camel and then on him, that what is good for camels is not good for poor old men and women.
- **Apparatus (pp.59–61):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 8 true/false; 10 short questions; 5 long questions (50–100 words); verb forms (10); **phrasal verbs (break in / out / off / down); punctuate the lines; passage comprehension (4 questions).**

### Lesson 10 — A Mild Attack of Locusts, Doris Lessing (printed pp.62–66)
- **Story (pp.62–63):** on a Rhodesian farm Margaret watches a locust swarm arrive: gongs and tin cans beaten, smoke fires lit around the seven mealie patches, neighbours telephoning warnings. The swarm settles like a moving brown blanket, trees snap under the weight, every blade is eaten; old Stephen explains the real fear — the egg-laden main swarm settling and the hopper years to come — yet the men eat supper with good appetites: "It could have been worse." (The printed text is an abridged form of Lessing's story.)
- **Apparatus (pp.63–66):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 10 short questions; 5 long questions (50–100 words); verb forms (5, with a worked example printed); punctuate the lines; **preposition fill-ins (at, on, off, in, over); passage comprehension.**

### Lesson 11 — I Have a Dream, Martin Luther King Jr. (printed pp.67–72)
- **Text (pp.67–68):** an excerpt of the 1963 Lincoln Memorial speech, printed from "I am not unmindful that some of you have come here out of great trials and tribulations" through the "Free at last" close, with the book's own printed Notes explaining the song "My country, 'tis of thee", the named mountains, and "Negro spiritual". An "About the Author" note (King, 1929–1968) follows. This is the book's only speech lesson.
- **Apparatus (pp.69–72):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 10 short questions (including the effect of repetition); 5 long questions (100–150 words); sentence joining; verb forms (10); punctuate the lines; **phrasal verbs (come out / off / in / down); passage comprehension (3 questions).**

### Lesson 12 — The Gift of the Magi, O. Henry (printed pp.73–78)
- **Story (pp.73–76):** printed in a simplified retelling of O. Henry's text. Della, with $1.87 saved, sells her knee-length hair for $20 and buys Jim a gold chain for his heirloom watch; Jim comes home having sold the watch to buy her the jewelled combs she had longed for — the gifts "too nice to use now", and the narrator's verdict that of all who give, these two were the wisest, the Magi. An "About the Author" note (O. Henry / William Sydney Porter, 1862–1910) follows.
- **Apparatus (pp.76–78):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 10 true/false; 10 short questions; 5 long questions (50–100 words); sentence-connection matching; punctuate the lines; **passage comprehension on the Magi passage (4 questions).**

### Lesson 13 — God Be Praised, Ahmed Nadeem Qasmi (printed pp.79–85)
- **Story (pp.79–84):** Maulvi Abul Barkat, once a comfortable village maulvi, sinks into poverty as his children multiply: shoes for the youngest at five rupees twelve annas, a vow to give up his powdered tobacco, bread and dal sent nightly by Chaudhry Fateh Dad. His whole savings — 43 rupees — go on seven yards of pink cloth for his daughter Mehrun's dowry suit at the new shop of Shamim Ahmed, who then comes in the dark to ask for Mehrun's hand himself; the Chaudhry adds gold pendants wrapped in a hundred-rupee note, and Mehrun is married in splendour. The story closes with the next daughters growing up and the Maulvi's faith — and gratitude to Allah — unshaken. An "About the Author" note (Qasmi, 1916–2006) precedes the exercises.
- **Apparatus (p.85):** a lighter exercise set than the other lessons — 10 short factual questions, then 10 "read the lesson and tick the correct word" items (choose the right word from three, and write the correct sentences in your notebook). No MCQs, true/false or verb-form exercises.

### Lesson 14 — Overcoat, Ghulam Abbas (printed pp.87–93)
- **Story (pp.87–91):** one January Saturday a dandified young man promenades on Lahore's Mall — brown overcoat, cream rose, silk scarf, polished cane — browsing a music shop, pricing a Persian carpet at 1,432 rupees, buying one cigarette with his single anna, apparently immune to the cold. Run down by a brick truck, he dies in hospital; undressed, he has no shirt or tie under the scarf, only a holed cotton sweater and a dirty vest, unmatched holed socks, and in the overcoat's pockets: a comb, a handkerchief, six annas and a few pies, a half-smoked cigarette, a small diary, the gramophone-record list and handbills. An "About the Author" note (Abbas, b. 1909, BBC; died November 1982) follows.
- **Apparatus (pp.91–93):** Theme; Reading Notes; Exercises — 10 MCQs; 8 short questions; 4 long answers "in not less than 100 words" (the articles in the pockets; the moral; a ten-line gist; the theme). No true/false or verb-form set in this lesson.

### Lesson 15 — The Angel and the Author – and Others, Jerome K. Jerome (printed pp.94–100)
- **Text (pp.94–96):** abridged, with bracketed editorial bridge-lines (e.g. "(The author tells of his good deeds.)", "(The Angel appears to have made a slight mistake.)"). A fortnight after Christmas the author dreams he has died and flies upward, meeting the Recording Angel with his bulky book; he eagerly audits his own Christmas goodness — the ten-shilling subscription (name misspelt once), four charity dinners, signed photographs for a bazaar, his Talbot Champneys performance for the Poor Curates' fund, balcony seats for the Destitute British show, the rummage-sale clothes, the motor-car raffle — while the Angel assures him everything has been noted, "together with other matters I, may be, had forgotten." An "About the Author" note (Jerome, b. 2 May 1859) follows.
- **Apparatus (pp.97–98):** the lighter pattern again — 5 short questions; a fill-in-the-blanks set with a ten-word bank (diary, weary, vexing, deeds, fortnight, recording, good, rummage, money, raffle); 10 "tick the correct word" items.

## 4. Language-work progression summary

This book teaches no grammar syllabus; its language work lives entirely inside the lesson exercises, and it follows a fixed drill diet rather than a progression:
- **In almost every full-pattern lesson:** "Use the correct form(s) of the verb(s) given in brackets" — 5–10 sentences lifted from the story, testing tense/form choice without any rule being stated (Lessons 1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 9, 10, 11) — and "Punctuate the following lines", an unpunctuated passage from the lesson's own text (Lessons 1–12 passim).
- **Vocabulary** is carried by the Reading Notes glossary of each lesson (hard words glossed in simple English) plus rotating "use the following words in your own sentences" items.
- **Rotating extras by lesson:** sentence-connection matching (L1, L6, L11, L12); indirect narration (L1 only); prepositions in own sentences / preposition fill-ins (L4, L5, L8, L10); phrasal-verb sets built on one verb (get in/out/on/off L6; sit/come sets L7; hit/ask sets L8; break set L9; come set L11); fill-in-the-blanks from the story (L7, L8); a reprinted-passage comprehension with questions (L5, L6, L7, L8, L9, L10, L11, L12).
- **Two lessons (13 and 15) drop the drill pattern** and use tick-the-correct-word and word-bank fill-ins instead; Lesson 14 drops true/false and verb forms.
- **Author apparatus:** "About the Author" notes appear for Dunsany (L6), Williams (L7), Sa'di (L8), King (L11), O. Henry (L12), Qasmi (L13), Abbas (L14) and Jerome (L15); the earlier lessons have none.

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 104 PDF pages (including a full-text search of the page-image OCR):
- **Tenses:** never taught — no tense names, paradigms or usage rules anywhere. The verb-form drills presuppose tense knowledge but explain nothing.
- **Passive voice:** never taught and never mentioned (the word "passive" does not occur in the book).
- **Conditionals:** never taught or named (the word "conditional" does not occur). Lesson 6's verb-form exercise happens to use if-sentences from the story, with no conditional pattern stated.
- **Reported speech:** not taught; it is *practised once*, without rules — Lesson 1, exercise 8, "Write down the following lines in indirect narration" (4 lines).
- **Relative clauses:** never taught or mentioned.
- **Also absent:** articles, modals, subject–verb agreement, comparison, question formation; no writing-skills teaching (the only writing is answering questions and "write the story in your own words"); no oral/listening work; no pronunciation work.
- **Examination preparation:** none — no past papers, no model paper, no pairing scheme, no marking scheme, no exam guidance anywhere in the book.
- **Answer keys:** none. No exercise, MCQ set or drill is answered anywhere in the book.

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments, with printed page)

- Lesson 5 text prints "Leaf and gravy dripping over the browned skin" — "Leaf" for "fat" (the standard translation reads "fat and gravy") — p.32.
- Lesson 10, MCQ iv asks about the sentence "And they neither went bankrupt nor got very rich" — that sentence does not occur in the story as printed in this book (the printed story text, pp.62–63, is an abridgement) — p.64.
- Lesson 6's "About the Author" calls Dunsany "one of the most charining of modern Irish writers" (for "charming") — p.40 [as read on the page image/OCR].
- Lesson 7's "About the Author" lists Williams's works as "Life Along the Passic" (for Passaic) and "Jn the Money" (for In the Money) — p.46 [as read on the page image/OCR].
- Contents page (PDF p.4) punctuates several entries with commas — "Lesson No, 2", "Lesson No.6", "Lesson No.9" — against "Lesson No. 1" elsewhere on the same page.

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **Scanned-image copy with watermark-only text layer:** this PDF is a page-image scan. Its embedded text layer contains ONLY the tiled diagonal third-party watermark "Freebooks.pk" (~900 characters per page of watermark fragments); no body text is extractable (pdftotext returns watermark letters only, on every page). The book was therefore read from the page images (OCR at 150 dpi renders/extracted page images, with visual checks of cover, title, contents and imprint pages).
- **Watermark:** "Freebooks.pk" is tiled diagonally across every page of this copy, including the cover (where a "More books" tag also appears). These are features of this PDF copy, not of the printed book.
- **Folio continuity:** unbroken — printed folios run 1–100 with no missing, duplicated or out-of-order pages; the book closes with the PCTB suggestions/imprint notice page (printed p.99: PCTB Lahore address, fax and e-mail, "Printed by: Maryah Printers Islamabad" — a different printer from the front imprint's Qudrat Ullah Printers, Lahore) and the back cover (PDF p.104, printed p.100 position) carrying only the board's name.
- **Adaptation features of this printing (not defects):** several texts are abridged or simplified in this book — The Piece of String with renamed characters and an added flood/tombstone ending (Lesson 5), The Gift of the Magi in simplified language (Lesson 12), an abridged locust story (Lesson 10), an excerpted speech (Lesson 11), and The Angel and the Author compressed with bracketed editorial bridge-lines (Lesson 15).

## Pakistan — English Book III, Grade 11 — Plays and Poems (file: g11-advance.pdf)

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed (cover):** "ENGLISH BOOK III" with a class badge "11" and the session "2018-19". **Title page (PDF p.3):** "ENGLISH BOOK – III / FOR / INTERMEDIATE CLASSES / (Plays and Poems)".
- **Publisher/board as printed (title page and cover):** "PUNJAB CURRICULUM AND TEXTBOOK BOARD, LAHORE".
- **Curriculum basis as printed (contents/imprint page, PDF p.4):** "Prepared and published vide NOC No. F7- 8/2003-English, Government of Pakistan, Ministry of Education (Curriculum Wing), Islamabad." No named national-curriculum statement appears — like Book I, this is the older PCTB intermediate book, in its 2018-19 printing.
- **Compilers as printed (PDF p.4):** Rafiq Mahmood; Bashir Ahmed Chaudhry; Razzi Abidi; Qazi Sajjad Ahmad; Mian Muhammad Afzal; Shahid Imtiaz. Supervised by: Safdir Hussain. Director (Manuscripts): Dr. Mobeen Akhtar. Layout: Hafiz Inam-ul-Haq. Deputy Director (Graphics)/Artist: Aisha Waheed.
- **Year/edition as printed (imprint, PDF p.4):** "DATE OF PRINTING: APRIL 2018; EDITION: 1ST; IMPRESSION: 20TH; NO OF COPIES: 18,000; PRICE: 40/-". **Published by: MALIK MUHAMMAD AFZAL & SONS, LAHORE; Printed by: QUDRAT ULLAH PRINTERS, LAHORE.** No ISBN is printed anywhere in the front matter of this copy. (A different printing of Book III circulates in other copies — see the coordinator's note in §7.)
- **Front matter (PDF p.2):** the same Quaid-e-Azam quotation page as Book I (Karachi, September 26, 1947), the national anthem in Urdu, and the PCTB security-sticker notice (this copy's sticker: Sr. No: 10345478, Code: B678243).
- **PDF page count:** 100 pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** printed page = PDF page − 4. Verified on foliated pages throughout (PDF p.5 = printed p.1, the first play page; PDF p.97 = printed p.93, Poem 20). Printed pagination runs 1–94; after printed p.94 come an unfoliated anti-corruption posters page (PDF p.99) and the back cover (PDF p.100). PDF pp.1–4 are cover, Quaid/anthem/sticker page, title page and contents/imprint page.

## 2. Architecture

A pure literature anthology in two parts, the companion to Book I's short stories: **Part-I (Plays)** — three one-act plays printed in full (pp.1–50) — and **Part-II (Poems)** — twenty short poems (pp.51–94). No units, no skills strands, no grammar course. Every play follows the same apparatus: banner page (play number, title, author, character list, setting) → the play text → **Theme** → **Glossary** → **EXERCISES**. Every poem follows a lighter version of it: banner page → poem text → **About the Poet** (only for some poems) → **Theme** → **Paraphrase** and/or **Glossary** → **EXERCISES** (headed EXERCISE in the singular for Poems 12, 14, 15, 17, 19, 20). ## 3. Item-by-item (printed page numbers)

- **Play 1 — Heat Lightning (pp.1–12).** Characters: Girl, First Man, Second Man; setting: the interior of a bus station on a stormy night. A young woman who has witnessed a murder and fled her stalled car takes refuge with a man waiting for the bus; a second man forces his way in and suspicion shifts between the two men until the twist ending — the "helper" is himself the killer, and a dog that comes in from the storm saves the girl (plot as printed; the book's own Theme calls it a melodrama/thriller built on terror, a dimly lit bus station, storm and lightning). **Theme** (p.9). **Glossary** (p.10): 25 entries from the dialogue ("hysterically — with outbursts of emotion" first). **Exercises** (pp.10–12): I. choose the correct answer (10 MCQs); II. mark statements true/false (8); III. answer the following questions (8); IV. answer in 100–150 words (5); V. punctuate the following lines; VI. fill in the blanks using a preposition given in bracket (8). No About-the-Author block for this play.
- **Play 2 — Visit to a Small Planet (pp.13–29).** Characters: Kreton, Roger Spelding, Ellen Spelding, Mrs. Spelding, John Randolph, General Powers, Aide. A television commentator's Maryland home receives a visitor from another planet — Kreton, a tourist for whom earth people are a hobby — and the army general who seals the house under martial law cannot touch him, read his ship, or stop him; Kreton announces he has come "to take charge" of the world as the curtain falls (plot summary only). **About the Author** (p.26): Gore Vidal, "born on October 3, 1925 in New York" (as printed), with Death in Fifth Position, Death Before Bed Time, A Star's Progress named. **Theme** (p.26). **Glossary** (pp.26–27): ~30 entries ("luminous — shining, giving out light" first). **Exercises** (pp.27–29): 1. choose the correct answer (10 MCQs); 2. mark true/false (10); 3. answer the following questions (10); 4. answer in 100–150 words (5, including character notes); 5. fill in the blanks (10, word-choice); 6. use the following phrasal verbs in your own sentences (pass on, pass through, pass over, pass down); 7. punctuate the following lines.
- **Play 3 — The Oyster and the Pearl (pp.30–50).** Characters: Harry Van Dusen, a barber; Clay Larrabee, a boy; Vivian McCutcheon, a new school teacher; Clark Larrabee, Clay's father; Man, a writer; Roxanna Larrabee; Greeley; Judge Applegarth; Wozzeck, a watch repairer. In Harry's barber shop in O.K.-by-the-Sea, California, young Clay believes the oyster he found holds a big pearl that will bring his runaway father home; the new teacher wants the oyster opened to teach the children truth, Harry protects the belief, the father secretly leaves money and returns, and a passing writer buys the unopened oyster for three hundred dollars (plot summary only). **Theme** (p.46): "a sentimental comedy… faith in the basic goodness of human nature". **Glossary** (pp.47–48): 31 numbered idiomatic or colloquial expressions "in the order in which they occur". **Exercises** (pp.48–50): I. write short answers to these questions (39); II. questions for discussion (7); III. WHO SAID IT? (identify the speaker of 6 quoted lines, to whom and on what occasion); IV. OPTIONAL ACTIVITY (two 100–150-word compositions). No About-the-Author block.
- **Poems 1–3 (pp.51–58).** Poem 1 **The Rain** (Davies): About the Poet (b.1871 Wales, d.1940, as printed), Theme (the rich leaves pass the drops to the poor beneath — a metaphor of rich and poor), Paraphrase; Exercises (p.52): MCQ (4), true/false (5), add two more rhyming words (3), critical appreciation, two open questions. Poem 2 **Night Mail** (Auden): About the Poet (b.1907, as printed), Theme (the mail coach's night journey), Glossary (5 entries); Exercises (pp.54–55): MCQ (4), true/false (6), rhyming words (3). Poem 3 **Loveliest of Trees, the Cherry Now** (Housman): About the Poet (1859–1936, as printed), Theme (life too short to relish beauty), Paraphrase (p.57), Glossary (4 entries); Exercises (pp.57–58): MCQ (6), true/false (10), rhyming words (3), explain-with-reference-to-context and three open questions.
- **Poems 4–6 (pp.59–65).** Poem 4 **O Where are You Going?** (Auden): Theme (the dialogue of reader and rider; courage and perseverance), Glossary (7 entries); Exercises (p.60): summarize the poem, answer questions (5), true/false (4) — no About the Poet. Poem 5 **In the Street of the Fruit Stalls** (Stallworthy): Theme (children forget misery in the pleasure of fruit), Paraphrase, Glossary (5); Exercises (pp.62–63): MCQ (4), true/false (4), rhyming words (4), write the missing word in each verse (4, MCQ), critical note, summary, four open questions, list of fruits. Poem 6 **A Sindhi Woman** (Stallworthy): Theme (a tribute to a working woman; the Karachi slums), Paraphrase, Glossary (pp.64–65); Exercises (p.65): rhyming words (4), missing rhyming words (4), summary, three open questions, MCQ (4), true/false (4).
- **Poems 7–9 (pp.66–72).** Poem 7 **Times** (Ecclesiastes extract): Theme only (everything happens at its appointed time); Exercises (p.67): explain the message of the first eight lines, complete the lines (5), antonyms (6: born, heaven, live, lose, weep, break), critical appreciation. Poem 8 **Ozymandias** (Shelley): About the Poet (1792–1822, as printed), Theme (pride and the levelling of time), Paraphrase (p.69), Glossary (10); Exercises (pp.69–70): MCQ (4), true/false (4), rhyming words (4), missing word in each verse (4), summary, two open questions. Poem 9 **The Feed** (Qasmi): About the Poet (b.1916 in Anga, Khushab District, as printed), Theme (a mother sparrow with one grain of millet for ten young ones), Glossary (p.72, 5 entries); Exercises (p.72): answer questions (4), MCQ (4), missing word (3), true/false (4), explain-with-reference-to-context, critical appreciation, one open question.
- **Poems 10–12 (pp.73–79).** Poem 10 **The Hollow Men** (Eliot — the printed text is the poem's first two sections only): About the Poet (Thomas Stearns Eliot, 1888–1965, as printed), Theme (modern men as soulless), Paraphrase (p.74), Glossary (5); Exercises (pp.74–75): MCQ (4), true/false (4), rhyming words (4), missing words (4), critical note, summary, two open questions. Poem 11 **Leisure** (Davies): Theme only; Exercises (p.77): complete the lines with rhyming words (3), the title question in 100–150 words, one open question, write the pairs of all rhyming words. Poem 12 **Ruba'iyat** (Iqbal): Glossary (3 entries), Theme (faith like Abraham's; Muslim harmony); EXERCISE (p.79, singular heading): answer the following questions (7). No About the Poet for Poems 11–12.
- **Poems 13–15 (pp.80–85).** Poem 13 **A Tale of Two Cities** (John Peter, as printed): Glossary (p.81, 2 entries: woeful, smashed), Theme (the two Japanese cities under the atomic bombs, rising again from the ashes); Exercises (p.81): six open questions plus more rhyming words (3). Poem 14 **My Neighbour Friend Breathing His Last!** (Bullah Shah, trans. A. R. Luther): Glossary (4 entries), Theme (p.83); EXERCISE (p.83): 7 questions including "Paraphrase the poem in your own words". Poem 15 **He Came to Know Himself** (Sachal Sarmast): Glossary (7 entries), Theme (p.85, self-knowledge and the love of Allah; Mansur on the gallows); EXERCISE (p.85): 6 items including "Explain these lines".
- **Poems 16–20 (pp.86–94).** Poem 16 **God's Attributes** (Rumi, trans. Dr. Nicholson): Glossary (3 entries in a row table), Theme (God's names as character-building and guidance); EXERCISES (p.86): 7 items, the last a homophone/sound-pair task (wait–weight given as the model). Poem 17 **The Delight Song** (Momaday): Theme; the prose explanation continues on p.88 above a 3-entry Glossary; EXERCISE (p.88): 7 questions. Poem 18 **Love – an Essence of All Religions** (Rumi, trans. Dr. Nicholson): Glossary (3), Theme; EXERCISES (p.90): two open questions, an antonym task (words taken from the poem: misfortune, stone, grief, sickness, wrath, dead, king against the printed example thorns–roses), and rhyming-word pairs from a given list. Poem 19 **A Man of Words and Not of Deeds** (Perrault, trans. Robert Samber): Glossary (3), Theme (p.92); EXERCISE (p.92): 6 items including paraphrase and "What is the moral of the poem?". Poem 20 **In Broken Images** (Graves): Glossary (2), Theme (remain inquisitive; mistrusting one's broken images makes one sharp); EXERCISE (p.94): 7 items, including "Rewrite the poem in past tense" and a detailed conclusion question.

## 4. Language-work progression summary

There is no language-work progression in the grammar sense: no tense, structure or usage is taught anywhere in the book. The language work that does exist rides entirely on the literature exercises, and it is the same small set recycled from item to item: **punctuation** of unpunctuated lines (Plays 1 and 2), **preposition/word-choice gap-fills** built from the play's own sentences (Plays 1 and 2), **phrasal verbs in own sentences** (Play 2: pass on/through/over/down), **rhyming words** (add two more; write the missing rhyming word; list the pairs — in most poem exercises), **antonyms** (Poem 7; Poem 18's antonym-from-the-poem task), **sound-similar word pairs** (Poem 16), one **rewrite the poem in past tense** task (Poem 20), and everywhere **summary writing, paraphrase, critical appreciation/critical note, explain-with-reference-to-context, and 100–150-word answers**. Vocabulary is carried wholly by the per-item Glossary tables of English meanings — never by translation into Urdu.

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 100 PDF pages (every page rendered and viewed):

- **Grammar of every kind:** never taught — no tenses, no passive voice, no conditionals, no reported speech as a system (the plays are printed as direct speech throughout and are never converted), no relative clauses, no articles, modals, comparison, subject–verb agreement or question formation. The only tense touch in the whole book is Poem 20's exercise "Rewrite the poem in past tense", which presupposes the knowledge it does not teach.
- **Writing skills:** no teaching of essays, letters, paragraphs or stories as forms; writing appears only as answers — summaries, paraphrases, critical appreciations, and 100–150-word answers about the set texts (plus Play 3's two optional compositions).
- **Speaking/listening/pronunciation:** none anywhere.
- **Examination preparation:** none — no model paper, no pairing scheme, no marking scheme, no past papers, no exam guidance. (Contrast the Grade 12 book, which carries a pairing scheme and model paper in its back matter.)
- **Answer keys:** none. No exercise in the book is answered in the book.
- **About-the-Poet coverage is partial:** biographical notes are printed for Poems 1, 2, 3, 8, 9 and 10 only; the other fourteen poems carry no poet note. Play 2 has an About-the-Author block; Plays 1 and 3 have none.
- **Text completeness:** Poem 10 prints only the first two sections of "The Hollow Men"; Poem 7 prints an extract of Ecclesiastes 3 (verses 1–12 as headed). Both are presented without any note that they are extracts (the Ecclesiastes heading itself gives the verse range).

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments, with printed page)

- Poem 13's Glossary (p.81) glosses only two words ("woeful", "smashed") — by far the shortest glossary in the book, against 5–10 entries elsewhere.
- Poem 16's Glossary (p.86) is set as a single-row table ("scare — terrify | discourse — talk | camphor — strong smelling substance") unlike the column tables used elsewhere.
- Play 2's About the Author (p.26) dates Gore Vidal's birth "October 3, 1925" — recorded as printed.
- The anti-corruption posters page after printed p.94 (PDF p.99) carries no folio, breaking the book's foliation at 94 although the PDF runs to 100 pages.
- (No further misprints were noted at the resolution of this read; the copy is a clean commercial scan apart from the watermark in §7.)

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **Scanned-image copy with watermark-only text layer:** this PDF is a page-image scan. Its embedded text layer contains ONLY the tiled diagonal third-party watermark "Freebooks.pk" (pdftotext returns watermark fragments only, on every page). The book was therefore read from the page images (100 dpi renders of all 100 pages, viewed as contact sheets, with single-page visual checks of the cover, title page, contents/imprint page and back matter).
- **Watermark:** "Freebooks.pk" is tiled diagonally across every page of this copy, including the cover (where a "More books" tag also appears). These are features of this PDF copy, not of the printed book.
- **Folio continuity:** printed folios run 1–94 continuously with no missing, duplicated or out-of-order content pages (every play and poem starts exactly where the contents page puts it); the posters page (PDF p.99) and back cover (PDF p.100) are unfoliated. The file ends with the back cover carrying only the board's seal and name.
- **Back-matter page (PDF p.99):** two National Accountability Bureau (Punjab) posters ("CORRUPTION LEADS TO DESTRUCTION" / "JOIN HANDS TO ELIMINATE CORRUPTION") above the PCTB suggestions notice ("Punjab Curriculum and Textbook Board provides standard textbooks at low price according to the approved curricula…"); the page's own footer reads "Printed by: Maryah Printers Islamabad" — a different printer from the front imprint's Qudrat Ullah Printers, Lahore.
- **Coordinator's note on printings (2026-11-04):** this section was read by the coordinator directly from the library's secured file (the copy whose imprint is given in §1: Malik Muhammad Afzal & Sons, April 2018, 20th impression). A second printing of Book III also circulates (an ILMI Kitab Khana, Lahore reprint was identified during this wave's set verification); the two printings carry the same PCTB contents, but the filing follows the secured file page-for-page, and any citation should be taken from this section.

## Pakistan — English, Grade 12 (file: g12.pdf)

Reader draft from a whole-book, page-by-page read. Page numbers below are PRINTED page numbers unless marked "PDF p." Summaries are my own words; quoted fragments are short headings/lines only. For stories/poems/novel only plot/theme summaries are given, never the text.

## 1. Identity block

- **Title exactly as printed (cover):** "ENGLISH 12"
- **Authority as printed (cover):** "PECTAA — PUNJAB EDUCATION, CURRICULUM, TRAINING AND ASSESSMENT AUTHORITY"
- **Curriculum basis as printed (imprint, PDF p.4):** "This textbook is based on Revised National Curriculum of Pakistan 2023"
- **Authors as printed (imprint):** Prof. Humayun Adil Hameed; Khawaja Mazhar-ul-Haq. Editor: Dr. Muhammad Akram. A five-member Review Committee (from GC University Faisalabad, Forman Christian College Lahore, Govt. Science College Wahdat Road Lahore, Govt. APWA College for Women Lahore) and PECTAA compliance/art-cell officers are named; Layout & Designing: Minal Tariq.
- **Year/edition/ISBN:** no year, no edition statement and no ISBN are printed on the cover or imprint page of this copy. (The exam-instruction pages at the back refer to a companion volume, "English Grammar and Composition 11-12 by PECTAA", for the essay/idiom/translation sections.)
- **PDF page count:** 165 pages.
- **Printed-page offset:** printed page = PDF page − 4. Verified across the book (PDF p.12 carries printed folio 8; PDF p.165 carries printed folio 161). Printed pagination runs 1–161; PDF pp.1–4 are cover, contents (2 pp.) and imprint/authors page. Each content page carries its folio twice in the text layer (page header and footer positions).

## 2. Architecture

Thirteen units, then an examination back-matter section. Each unit follows the pattern: learning-outcomes list → pre-reading questions → main text (story, poem, essay, travelogue, or — Unit 13 — a complete short novel) with while-reading margin questions → theme note → author note → glossary → "Reading and Critical Thinking" (open questions + MCQs) → "Vocabulary and Grammar" (one named language topic per unit) → "Oral Communication Skills" → "Writing Skills" → occasional "For the Teacher" notes. The two-page contents table (PDF pp.2–3) lists each unit's theme and its reading, vocabulary/grammar, oral and writing strands with a page number (on the reliability of those page numbers see §7).

Unit map (printed start pages verified at each unit heading):

| Unit | Title | Author | Printed start |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Journey to Taif | Sayyid Abul Hasan Ali Hasani Nadwi | 1 |
| 2 | The Last Lesson | Alphonse Daudet | 8 |
| 3 | On His Blindness (Poem) | John Milton | 17 |

| 12 | A Visit to the Swat Valley | H. P. Stewart | 88 |
| 13 | The Pearl (Novel) | John Steinbeck | 97 |
| — | Pairing Scheme / Model Paper ("Instructions for Preparation of Exam Paper of English for Intermediate Part II" + "Model Paper English-XII") | — | 156 |

## 3. Unit-by-unit

### Unit 1 — Journey to Taif, Sayyid Abul Hasan Ali Hasani Nadwi (printed pp.1–7) [read from page images — this unit has no usable text layer in this PDF; see §7]
- **Text (pp.1–4):** prose account of the Prophet Muhammad's (PBUH) journey to Taif (theme: Ethics and Values / Tolerance). Summary: after the deaths of his uncle Abu Talib and Hazrat Khadija (RA) and the Quraysh boycott, he sets out on foot in 619–620 CE with his adopted son Hazrat Zayd bin Haritha (RA) to preach Tawheed at Taif; the Banu Thaqif chiefs — 'Abdulayl, Mas'ud and Habeeb — mock and reject the message; at the chiefs' instigation slaves and street children stone him until blood clots in his shoes; in the orchard of 'Otba and Shayba their Christian slave Addaas brings grapes, and the "Bismillah" exchange and the mention of Hazrat Yunus of Naynawa (Nineveh) move Addaas to kiss his head, hands and feet; carried unconscious out of the city by Zayd, he makes the famous Taif supplication (quoted in full); when Jibreel and the Angel of the Mountains offer to crush the town, he refuses and hopes their descendants will worship Allah alone. Theme paragraph (unwavering faith, patience, compassion, forgiveness over vengeance) and an author note on Nadwi (Nadwatul Ulama scholar, author of *Islam and the World* and *Saviours of Islamic Spirit*, d. 1999) follow; glossary begins (arrogance, befit, eagerness, feebleness, instigated, miserable, oppressed, resided, serious…).
- **Reading work (pp.5–6):** 8 open questions; 8 MCQs; then a taught read-aloud skill — correct pronunciation, appropriate pitch and voice variation (with a worked dialogue example) — plus two reflective questions on tone.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.7):** use of idioms and proverbs in different texts — idiom vs proverb defined ("face the music"; "Actions speak louder than words"), with two short questions. No other grammar is taught in this unit.
- **Oral (p.7):** critical analysis of situations or events — defined (causes, consequences, motivations, perspectives), applied to the question of why the Prophet (PBUH) forgave the people of Taif.
- **Writing (p.7):** clear and coherent writing — defined with a clear-vs-incoherent example pair, plus one reflective question.

### Unit 2 — The Last Lesson, Alphonse Daudet (printed pp.8–16)
- **Text (pp.8–12):** the complete short story. Plot summary: in Alsace after the Franco-Prussian war, schoolboy Franz arrives late to find his French teacher M. Hamel in Sunday clothes and the village elders on the back benches: an order from Berlin makes this the last French lesson before German-only teaching. Franz's regret, Hamel's speech on guarding one's language, the writing lesson, and the closing gesture — "Vive La France!" written on the board — end the story. Theme note (language as identity/national pride) and author note follow; glossary (anxious, apprentice, dreadful, embroidered, nuisance, scolding…).
- **Reading work (pp.13–14):** 10 open questions; 10 MCQs; an opinion paragraph task; a taught section on narrative structure analysis (beginning in the middle, tragic endings, circular structure — with Odyssey, Romeo and Juliet, Of Mice and Men as examples) applied to the story.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.14–15):** adjectival, prepositional and adverbial phrases defined with examples, then identified in a set paragraph.
- **Oral (p.15):** active-listening exercise (teacher reads, students summarise in turn).
- **Writing (pp.15–16):** editing vs proofreading taught with examples, then a deliberately error-filled paragraph on intelligence to edit/proofread — followed immediately by the full corrected version printed on the same spread (p.16), i.e. this exercise's answer is given in the book.

### Unit 3 — On His Blindness, John Milton (printed pp.17–22)
- **Text (p.18):** Milton's sonnet printed in full (14 lines). Theme: the blind poet fears his "one talent" is useless; Patience answers that God does not need man's work — "They also serve who only stand and wait." Poet biography follows; glossary (chide, ere, lodged, murmur, talent…).
- **Reading work (pp.19–20):** 9 open questions (the meaning of "my light is spent", why the poet fears his talent is useless, who answers his doubt, "bear his mild yoke", the final line's meaning, the poem's central message); 8 MCQs; a taught section on critical interpretation/critiquing readings, with a question on supporting interpretations with evidence. (The 9 questions are printed on p.19 but are missing from this PDF's text layer — verified on the page image; see §7.)
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.20–21):** using context to clarify meaning; recognising word patterns (prefix un-, suffix -ful, prefix re-); using reference materials (dictionary/glossary/thesaurus); verifying a preliminary meaning — each taught with an example and one short question.
- **Oral (p.21):** asking/answering questions for communicative purposes; pair-work reporter/entrepreneur interview role-play.
- **Writing (p.22):** final-draft writing and critiquing (revise/edit before the final draft; what to check when critiquing).

### Unit 4 — The Power of Digital Learning (printed pp.23–32)
- **Text (pp.23–27):** original expository essay on MOOCs (features, flexibility, certificates, low completion rates), Twitter as a "micro-university of ideas", and LinkedIn (profiles, networking, jobs, LinkedIn Learning); how the three work together; digital literacy and responsibility with tips for students; future of education. Glossary (acquire, adaptability, collaborate, concise, dynamic, integration, visibility…).
- **Reading work (pp.28–29):** 10 open questions; 10 MCQs (several with visibly truncated/garbled option sets — see §6); a taught section on analysing organisational patterns (list/sequence, comparison–contrast, cause–effect, problem–solution, reasons–conclusion).
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.30–31):** denotative vs connotative meaning with a word table (snake, home, cheap, slim/skinny) and a 14-word activity paragraph (towering, musty, piercing, crooked, stingy…).
- **Oral (p.31):** using complex questions for different audiences (peers/professionals/community) — group interview role-play.
- **Writing (pp.31–32):** writing with correct mechanics (punctuation, capitalisation, spelling, grammar, formatting); developing and strengthening writing (planning, revising, editing, rewriting, trying a new approach) with a worked speech example.

### Unit 5 — The Giving Tree, Shel Silverstein (printed pp.33–38)
- **Text (pp.34–35):** the complete picture-book story in prose form. Plot summary: a tree gives a boy her leaves/ shade in childhood, then her apples to sell, her branches for a house, and her trunk for a boat; as an old man he returns and she, now a stump, offers him a seat — "and the tree was happy." Theme note (unconditional love/selfless sacrifice, and one-sided taking) and author note; glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.36–37):** 10 open questions; 5 MCQs; a taught section on inference and deduction in reading with a worked example and an inference task on a short "old house" text.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.37):** figurative language, word relationships and nuances in meaning (oxymorons in Romeo and Juliet as the worked example); one analytical question.
- **Oral (p.38):** confident/fluent speaking — group choice of conflict resolution, panel discussion, role-play or dialogue, with peer feedback.
- **Writing (p.38):** narrative writing — turn a real-life experience into a structured narrative.

### Unit 6 — The Fun They Had, Isaac Asimov (printed pp.39–44)
- **Text (pp.39–42):** the complete short story. Plot summary: on 17 May 2155 Margie Jones and Tommy find a printed book about old schools; Margie, who hates her strict mechanical teacher (recently repaired and re-set by the County Inspector), daydreams about the old schools where children learned together from human teachers — "the fun they had." Theme note and Asimov biography; glossary (attic, betcha, loftily, nonchalantly, sector…).
- **Reading work (p.43):** 8 open questions; 5 MCQs.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.43):** using appropriate words and phrases for the situation; one question drawing examples from the story.
- **Oral (p.44):** speak fluently on mechanical vs human teachers.
- **Writing (p.44):** informative/explanatory writing — a 5–6-sentence paragraph on how Margie's school differs from today's schools.

### Unit 7 — Because I could Not Stop for Death, Emily Dickinson (printed pp.45–49)
- **Text (p.46):** the poem printed in full (six stanzas). Theme summary: Death, personified as a polite carriage driver, takes the speaker past school, fields and setting sun to the grave-"house", toward eternity; centuries afterwards feel shorter than that day. "About the poetess" biography; glossary (cornice, eternity, haste, immortality, quivering, surmised…).
- **Reading work (p.48):** 10 open questions; 5 MCQs (synonym-style answer options include immortality/friend/teacher for carriage companions).
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.48–49):** parts of speech — the eight categories named and exemplified in one analysed sentence (noun, pronoun, verb, adjective, adverb, preposition, conjunction, interjection). No exercises beyond the example analysis are printed in this unit's grammar slot.
- **Oral (p.49):** dramatic techniques (soliloquy as the worked example); identify the technique in a given situation.
- **Writing (p.49):** writing arguments with valid reasoning and evidence (claim/reasoning/evidence model on school uniforms); write a 3–4-sentence argument on daily homework.

### Unit 8 — The Devoted Friend, Oscar Wilde (printed pp.50–61)
- **Text (pp.50–58):** the complete fairy tale. Plot summary: the Linnet tells the Water-rat of little Hans, whose rich "devoted friend" the Miller praises friendship endlessly while taking Hans's flowers, plank, labour and errands against a perpetually promised broken wheelbarrow; sent on a stormy night to fetch the doctor without even a lent lantern, Hans drowns on the moor; the Miller mourns theatrically and regrets his "generosity". Theme note (true vs false friendship) and Wilde biography; glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.59–60):** 8 open questions; 5 MCQs.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.60):** sentence structure — subject and predicate, with one correct/incorrect example pair and one identification question.
- **Oral (p.60):** engaging effectively in collaborative discussions; one reflective question.
- **Writing (p.61):** descriptive composition — describe your classroom using at least three senses.

### Unit 9 — The Doll's House, Katherine Mansfield (printed pp.62–72)
- **Text (pp.62–68):** the complete short story. Plot summary: the Burnell girls receive a splendid doll's house and are allowed to invite schoolmates, two at a time, to see it — but the Kelvey sisters, daughters of a washerwoman and a jailed father, are excluded by children and adults alike; Kezia defies the ban and shows them the house, cherishing its little lamp, before Aunt Beryl drives the Kelveys out; walking away, Else's whole consolation is "I seen the little lamp." Theme note (class discrimination) and Mansfield biography; glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.69–70):** 10 analytical open questions (symbolism of the paint smell and the doll's house, silence as oppression, Kezia vs Aunt Beryl, modern parallels…) and 5 analytical MCQs — the most demanding question set in the book.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.71):** "Spot the Error" — 15 faulty sentences to correct (e.g. pronoun order, "yours' sincerely", double comparative "very better"), WITH a printed KEY on the same page giving the corrected words — the only full answer key to a grammar exercise in the book.
- **Oral (p.71):** role-play discussion — Kezia, Isabel, Else Kelvey, Aunt Beryl and a narrator each explain the courtyard incident to the teacher.
- **Writing (p.72):** creative writing — gather textual "evidence" about the Kelvey sisters, then write a one-page diary entry in character.

### Unit 10 — All the World's a Stage, William Shakespeare (printed pp.73–80)
- **Text (p.74):** Jaques's speech from As You Like It printed in full (seven ages: infant, schoolboy, lover, soldier, justice, pantaloon, second childishness). Central-idea note and Shakespeare biography (which lists Romeo and Juliet among his "comedies" — see §6); glossary (mewling, oblivion, pard, sans, satchel, treble…).
- **Reading work (pp.76–77):** 9 open questions; 7 synonym MCQs on underlined poem words.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.77–79):** the book's fullest language section — literary devices (metaphor, simile, imagery, repetition, personification, irony), each with a line reference from the poem plus an example from another named poem (Burns, Wordsworth, Keats, Tennyson, Shelley, Gray); then "Grammatical Aspects" drawn from the poem: simple present tense, possessive/genitive, simile structure "like + noun", participle phrases, ellipsis, repetition of "sans", parallel structure, contractions.
- **Oral (p.80):** a printed dialogue (Jahanzeb/Hunzala) about the poem, for group read-aloud practice.
- **Writing (p.80):** write your own modern "Stages of Life" paragraph (120–150 words) using a metaphor, a simile and descriptive language.

### Unit 11 — A Letter to God, Gregorio Lopez Fuentes (printed pp.81–87)
- **Text (pp.81–83):** the complete short story. Plot summary: hail destroys Lencho's crop; he writes to God asking for 100 pesos; the postmaster and staff collect and send just over half, signed "GOD"; Lencho, unsurprised by the miracle but angered by the shortfall, writes again asking for the rest — but not through the mail, "because the post office employees are a bunch of crooks." Theme note (faith, collaboration, irony) and author note; glossary.
- **Reading work (pp.84–85):** 10 open questions; 10 vocabulary-in-context MCQs (crest, dotted, mortified, contentment, re-sow, resolution, unyielding, downpour, conscientious, crooks).
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (p.85):** a second vocabulary MCQ set (7 items on words from the story: exclaimed, "frozen pearls" as metaphor, amiable, unshakable, desolate, conscience, contentment, petition). (No separate grammar topic is taught in this unit beyond vocabulary.)
- **Oral (p.86):** group discussion on how unshakeable faith and human compassion interact in the story.
- **Writing (p.87):** analyse the two central ideas; write an objective summary with transitional devices; write a 4–5-sentence paragraph as the postmaster after the second letter.

### Unit 12 — A Visit to the Swat Valley, H. P. Stewart (printed pp.88–96)
- **Text (pp.88–92):** first-person travelogue, complete. Summary: a spring motor journey over the Malakand Pass into Swat — valley scenery, Gujar travellers, olive trees in graveyards, Buddhist stupas and monasteries, Saidu Sharif and Mingora, a rock-carved Buddha (about twenty-five feet), a wedding procession with the bride in a doli, bazaar shopping for Swat blankets, rain cutting the trip short at the Pir Baba road, and the closing cinema metaphor (a film in two parts, sunshine and rain, with a regretted unseen "part three"). Theme note and author note (which uses "her" for the author in the unit questions context and "his" in the note — see §6); glossary (bas-relief, bedraggled, doli, dowry, precipices, schist, stupa…).
- **Reading work (pp.93–94):** 10 analytical open questions (purpose, tone shift, the "giant's castle" metaphor, first-person viewpoint, the cinema ending…); 8 MCQs.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.95):** 5 vocabulary-in-context MCQs (exclaim, precipices, stormed, surmounted, slopes). (No grammar topic is taught in this unit.)
- **Oral (p.95):** listening-and-response to a read-aloud passage; paraphrase a landscape description with a partner; discuss Stewart's purpose/tone vs a modern travel blog.
- **Writing (p.96):** analytical paragraph (150–200 words) critiquing Stewart's outsider/colonial-era viewpoint with textual references; write a précis of the article.

### Unit 13 — The Pearl (Novel), John Steinbeck (printed pp.97–155)
- **Text (pp.97–144):** Steinbeck's novella printed complete, Chapters 1–6, with the author's framing prologue. Plot summary: pearl-fisher Kino's baby Coyotito is stung by a scorpion; the town doctor refuses treatment without money; Kino dives and finds "the Pearl of the World"; the doctor, priest, buyers and neighbours converge on him; the buyers collude to undervalue the pearl; night attacks, a killing in self-defence, the burning of his house and breaking of his canoe drive the family to flee north; trackers follow them to a mountain spring, and in the night fight Coyotito is killed by a rifle shot; Kino and Juana return side by side and Kino throws the pearl back into the sea.
- **Critical apparatus (pp.144–145):** a Marxist-critical reading of the novella supplied by the textbook (colonial capitalism, alienation, false consciousness, commodification, reification) and a long Steinbeck biography (1962 Nobel Laureate; Tortilla Flat, The Grapes of Wrath, Ed Ricketts).
- **Glossary (pp.145–146):** about 50 words (avarice, bulwark, cozened, dissembling, estuary, furtive, germane, incandescence, indigent, judicious, keening, lateen, leprosy, malignant, monolithic, parable, poultice, pulque, subjugation, transfigured, tulles…).
- **Reading work (pp.147–151):** 32 open questions, all framed in critical-theory terms (alienation, false consciousness, cultural hegemony, commodification, class consciousness…); 25 MCQs in the same frame.
- **Vocabulary/Grammar (pp.152–153):** antonyms table (about 27 words) to complete and use in sentences; synonyms table (about 27 words); then **prepositions** taught in six types (time, place, direction, manner/means/agent, cause/purpose, concession) with usage nuances, closing with a 15-item fill-in exercise using advanced collocations (yield to, given to, militate against, acquitted himself…, impeached for, distaste for, incompatible with, beyond the horizon, peculiar in, deliberated on, independent of, indifferent to, fixed on, robbed of, invests…in).
- **Oral (p.154):** "The Trial of Kino" — a structured mock-trial role-play debate (prosecution, defence, witnesses, jury, judge) on whether Kino is guilty of destroying the community's hope.
- **Writing (p.155):** argumentative paragraph on whether Kino could have kept the pearl without tragedy; construct a dialogue between two friends analysing modern colonisation and manufactured needs.

### Back matter — Pairing Scheme / Model Paper (printed pp.156–161)
- **"Instructions for Preparation of Exam Paper of English for Intermediate Part II" (pp.156–157):** the pairing scheme for the Grade 12 English paper — 100 marks; Objective 20 marks / 30 minutes (10 synonym MCQs drawn by unit groups, 5 preposition MCQs, 5 correct-sentence MCQs); Subjective 80 marks / 2:30 hours (Q2 short questions from Units 1, 2, 4, 5, 6 — any 5 of 8; Q3 from Units 8, 9, 11, 12 — any 5 of 8; Q4 from Unit 13 — any 7 of 11; Q5 stanza explanation from Units 3, 7, 10; Q6 essay, Q7 idioms and Q8 Urdu→English translation all taken from the companion "English Grammar and Composition 11-12 by PECTAA").
- **"Model Paper English-XII" (pp.158–161):** a full model objective paper (20 items with bubble-sheet format: 10 synonym items, 5 preposition items, 5 correct-sentence items) and the subjective paper (Q2–Q8 exactly on the pairing scheme, ending with an essay choice — Pollution / Patriotism / My Hobby / Smog — idioms list, and the translation slot, with the English-medium alternative paragraph on "My Hobby"). **No answers or marking scheme for the model paper are printed.**

## 4. Grammar / vocabulary progression summary

This book does **not** run a systematic grammar course. Each unit carries one light "Vocabulary and Grammar" slot, and the sequence is: idioms and proverbs (U1) → phrases: adjectival/prepositional/adverbial (U2) → context clues, word patterns, reference materials, verifying meaning (U3) → denotation/connotation (U4) → figurative language and nuance (U5) → appropriate word choice (U6) → parts of speech (U7) → sentence structure: subject/predicate (U8) → spot-the-error correction practice (U9) → literary devices + grammar points mined from the poem (U10) → vocabulary in context only (U11, U12) → antonyms, synonyms and a full prepositions lesson (U13). Vocabulary work is otherwise carried by the per-unit glossaries and synonym-MCQ practice, and it is the vocabulary strand — not grammar — that the back-matter exam scheme actually tests (synonyms, prepositions, correct sentences).

## 5. Honest negatives — what this book never teaches

Checked systematically across all 165 PDF pages:
- **Tenses:** no tense system is taught at all — no present/past/future paradigms, no perfect or continuous forms as a topic. The only tense content in the book is one "Grammatical Aspects" entry in Unit 10 noting that the poem uses the simple present for universal truths.
- **Passive voice:** never taught or mentioned as a topic anywhere.
- **Conditionals:** never taught — no conditional types, no if-clause patterns (the word "conditional" does not appear as a grammar topic).
- **Reported speech:** never taught — no direct/indirect speech section anywhere.
- **Relative clauses:** never taught — no relative-clause or relative-pronoun section (clauses as such are not taught; Unit 8 stops at subject/predicate, Unit 2 at phrases).
- **Also absent:** modal verbs; articles; subject–verb agreement rules; question and negative formation; comparison of adjectives; punctuation as a taught topic (it appears only inside "correct mechanics" advice, U4).
- **Examination preparation:** present but limited — the back matter gives the pairing scheme and one full model paper (pp.156–161). There are **no past papers**, **no marking scheme for the model paper's answers**, and **no exam strategy/technique guidance**; essay, idiom and translation practice are expressly outsourced to the companion grammar book.
- **Answer keys:** almost none. The only answers printed in the book are (a) the corrected version of the Unit 2 editing/proofreading paragraph (p.16) and (b) the KEY to Unit 9's Spot-the-Error exercise (p.71). The model paper and all other exercises carry no key.

## 6. Slips / misprints as printed (short fragments, with printed page)

- Unit 1 read-aloud section defines its skill with a garbled sentence: "This skill involves reading means reading aloud clearly and fluently" — p.6.
- Model paper, Subjective Q.2: items iv and v are the identical question printed twice — "How does Twitter act as a 'micro-university of ideas'?" — p.160.
- Unit 10 author note lists "comedies like As You Like It and Romeo and Juliet" — Romeo and Juliet classed as a comedy — p.75.
- Unit 4 MCQ 1 options are garbled/truncated: "massive open online courses / massive open / major online / modern open" — p.28; MCQ 2 option "Coursera EdX" merges two platforms and option c reads "LinkedInj obs" — p.28.
- Unit 12 text: "persian lilace" (lilac), "a range of Show mountains" (Snow), "gally clad dancers" (gaily), "very ugly things and so under becoming" (unbecoming), "the pass over Malakand had seemed scary It when we were going" — pp.89–92 passim.
- Unit 12 framing is inconsistent about the author: the unit's writing question says "critique his position… he, as a colonial-era British author" while an oral question refers to "Stewart's description" with "his text", and the reading question 1 asks about "her visit" — pp.93–96.
- Unit 8 text prints "Jn the winter, also, he was extremely lonely" (for "In the winter") — p.51.
- Contents table (PDF p.3) gives the Unit 13 vocabulary strand page as "151" in a column of unit start pages, and its start-page numbers drift from the actual unit starts from Unit 4 onward (see §7).

## 7. Copy defects of this PDF file

- **Watermark:** a diagonal third-party watermark "www.taleem360.com" runs across the pages of this copy (its letters — w w w . ta le em 36 0 . co m — are embedded in the text layer of most pages, including the cover). This is a feature of this PDF copy, not of the printed book.
- **Missing text layer (pages present as images):** printed pp.1–4 and 6 (PDF pp.5–8 and 10) carry no body text layer — pdftotext returns only watermark letters and the folio; PDF p.9 (printed p.5) and PDF p.11 (printed p.7) return only headings/folio fragments. These are Unit 1's pages; they are present and legible as page images, and folio continuity is unbroken (printed 1–7 run into Unit 2 at printed 8). Unit 1 was therefore read from the page images.
- **Unit 3, printed p.19, partial text-layer gap:** the 9 open questions under "A. Answer the following questions:" are printed on the page but do not come out in pdftotext extraction of this copy (the extraction jumps from the heading to the teacher note). Verified present on the rendered page image; the printed page itself is complete.
- **Contents page numbers unreliable:** the contents table's "Page No." column (PDF p.3) matches actual unit starts only for Units 1–3 (1, 8, 17); from Unit 4 onward the printed column (22, 32, 38, 44, 49, 60, 70, 78, 84, 92) falls increasingly behind the actual starts (23, 33, 39, 45, 50, 62, 73, 81, 88, 97), and the last row's "151" is the start of Unit 13's question section, not of its vocabulary strand. Unit starts in §2/§3 are taken from the unit heading pages themselves.
- **Otherwise:** no missing, duplicated or out-of-order pages found; printed folios run continuously 1–161; the file ends with the model paper's last page (printed 161) — no separate back cover page.
